Moving vectors and core blocks of Ariki-Koike algebras Thanks: The third author thanks Joseph Chuang for his useful comments on the affine Weyl group, moving vectors and Rouquier blocks.
Yanbo Li, Xiangyu Qi, Kai Meng Tan

Abstract
. We classify the core blocks of Ariki-Koike algebras by their moving vectors. Using this classification, we obtain a necessary and sufficient condition for Scopes equivalence between two core blocks, and express the number of simple modules lying in a core block as a classical Kostka number. Under certain conditions on the multicharge and moving vector, we further relate the graded decomposition numbers of these blocks in characteristic zero to the graded decomposition numbers of the Iwahori-Hecke algebras of type A A .



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
20C08, 05E10

1.
Introduction

The Ariki-Koike algebra, introduced in the early 1990s by Susumu Ariki and Kazuhiko Koike
[
AK94
]
, is a significant family of algebras that generalises the well-known Iwahori-Hecke algebras of type
A
.
It arises in the context of the study of symmetries in the representation theory of Hecke algebras and categorification of quantum groups, and
is intimately connected with the category
\mathcal{O}
for affine Lie algebras, as well as with the representation theory of

\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
and other Lie theoretic structures.
Its representation theory has deep connections with various important mathematical objects and phenomena, such as Young tableaux, the theory of symmetric functions, Lusztig’s theory of cell modules and Kazhdan-Lusztig polynomials.
The more recent introduction of the Khovanov-Lauda-Rouquier (KLR) algebras has endowed the Ariki-Koike algebras with a
\mathbb{Z}
-grading, providing new insights into their structure. In particular, the
v
-decomposition numbers arising from the canonical basis of the Fock space representation of the quantum affine algebra
U_{v}(\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{e})
are proved to be the graded decomposition numbers of Ariki-Koike algebras in characteristic zero
[
BK09
]
, generalising the theorem of Ariki for ungraded decomposition numbers
[
Ari96
]
.

Core blocks of Ariki-Koike algebras are first defined by Fayers
[
Fay07
]
.
These are blocks for which the multipartitions lying in them are all multicores, and are believed to be the simplest non-simple blocks.
Despite their perceived simplicity, these blocks can have arbitrarily large weight, and the research conducted so far has focused on blocks with very small weight.
In fact the authors are not aware of any work on these blocks with level
\ell\geq 3
and weight
k\geq 4
.

An important equivalence between the blocks of Ariki-Koike algebras is the Scopes equivalence.
This is a special type of Morita equivalence in which the correspondence between distinguished modules such as the Specht modules or the simple modules can be easily described.
In particular,
Scopes equivalence preserves decomposition numbers.
However,
determining when two blocks are Scopes equivalent is generally a challenging problem, even for level 1, i.e. the Iwahori-Hecke algebras of type
A
.
Dell’Arciprete
[
Del24
]
and the first and third authors
[
LT24
]
provided some different sufficient conditions for two blocks to be Scopes equivalent.
However, as noted in
[
LT24
, Remark 4.15]
, neither condition is necessary for two core blocks to be Scopes equivalent.

The concept of moving vectors was first introduced by the first and second authors
[
LQ23
]
to classify the blocks of Ariki-Koike algebras with finite representation type.
This landmark idea plays a far more significant role in controlling the representation theory of the blocks than their weights,
and it serves a function similar to the weights of blocks in Iwahori-Hecke algebras of type
A
for the higher-level Ariki-Koike algebras.
For their purposes, moving vectors were only defined in
[
LQ23
]
for the so-called FLOTW multicharges, where the components in each multicharge are increasing, with the last no more than
e
larger than the first.
Here, we provide a slightly different perspective to this groundbreaking concept and generalise it to any arbitrary multicharge.

In this paper, we present a new understanding of the core blocks of Ariki-Koike algebras through their moving vectors.
Our first main result (Theorem
3.17
) establishes that a block of an Ariki-Koike algebra is a core block if and only if its moving vector has a zero component.
Using this classification of core blocks, we quickly determine that the connected components of the weight graph defined by Lyle and Ruff in
[
LR16
]
are the ‘same’ as those of the undirected graph that arises naturally from its moving vector (Theorem
3.21
), and provide a straightforward method (Corollary
3.22
) to determine if a multipartition is decomposable in the sense of Fayers
[
Fay07
]
.

Our characterisation of core blocks by their moving vectors yields several important results, as the multicores lying in them have very special properties.
We exploit this to obtain our second main result (Theorem
4.24
): a simple necessary and sufficient condition for two core blocks to be Scopes equivalent.
In particular, this demonstrates that, for level 2, all core blocks lying in the same affine Weyl group orbit are all Scopes equivalent.
This perhaps explain why the core blocks of Iwahori-Hecke algebras of type
B
(i.e. level 2) are more tractable than others.

In our investigation of Scopes equivalence between core blocks, we encounter blocks in every Scopes equivalence class that have extremely rich properties.
Using these blocks, we derive our third main result (Theorem
5.8
), which expresses the number of simple modules in a core block as a classical Kostka number.

All the above results are valid for arbitrary multicharge.
Our final main result (Theorem
5.12
) however requires some condition on the multicharge and only applies to a subset of core blocks with such multicharge.
For these blocks, Theorem
5.12
establishes a relationship between their
v
-decomposition numbers and those in level one.

We now indicate the organisation of this paper. After providing the necessary background in the next section, we look at the moving vectors in Section
3
. Section
4
is dedicated to a closer examination of the core blocks, where we provide a necessary and sufficient condition for two core blocks to be Scopes equivalent. In Section
5
, we relate the number of simple modules lying in a core block to a classical Kostka number, as well as the
v
-decomposition numbers of core blocks satisfying certain conditions to those in level
1
.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Common notations and conventions

Throughout,
\mathbb{Z}^{+}
denotes the set of positive integers.
For
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, we write

\mathbb{Z}_{<k}:=\{x\in\mathbb{Z}\mid x<k\},

and similarly for
\mathbb{Z}_{\leq k}
,
\mathbb{Z}_{>k}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq k}
.
For
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
,

[a,\,b]:=\{x\in\mathbb{Z}\mid a\leq x\leq b\}.

We fix
e,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
with
e\geq 2
.
For
m\in\{e,\ell\}
, we write
a\equiv_{m}b
for
a\equiv b\pmod{m}
.
We also identify
\mathbb{Z}/m\mathbb{Z}
with
[0,\,m-1]
.

For
\mathbf{t}=(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{m})\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
, write

|\mathbf{t}|:=\sum_{i=1}^{m}t_{i}.

In addition

\boldsymbol{1}:=(1,\dotsc,1)\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}.

Of course,
\boldsymbol{1}
depends on
m
, which will always be clear from the context in this paper.
Moreover, for
x\in\mathbb{Z}
with
x=am+b
, where
a\in\mathbb{Z}
and
b\in\mathbb{Z}/m\mathbb{Z}
, define

\mathbf{v}_{m}(x):=(\overbrace{a,\dotsc,a}^{m-b\text{ times}},\overbrace{a+1,\dotsc,a+1}^{b\text{ times}})\in\mathbb{Z}^{m},

which satisfies
|\mathbf{v}_{m}(x)|=x
.

Furthermore,
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathtt{p}}
denotes the indicator function on the statement
\mathtt{p}
, i.e.
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathtt{p}}=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if $\mathtt{p}$ is true};\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

2.2.
\beta
-sets and abaci

A
\beta
-set
\mathsf{B}
is a subset of
{\mathbb{Z}}
such that both
\mathrm{max}(\mathsf{B})
and
\mathrm{min}({\mathbb{Z}}\setminus\mathsf{B})
exist. Its charge
\mathcal{s}(\mathsf{B})
is defined as

\mathcal{s}(\mathsf{B}):=|\mathsf{B}\cap{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}|-|{\mathbb{Z}}_{<0}\setminus\mathsf{B}|.

We denote the set of all
\beta
-sets by
\mathcal{B}
and the set of all
\beta
-sets with charges
s
by
\mathcal{B}(s)
. Clearly,
\mathcal{B}=\bigcup_{s\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\mathcal{B}(s)
(disjoint union).

Let
\mathsf{B}\in\mathcal{B}
. The
\infty
-abacus display of
\mathsf{B}
is a horizontal line with positions labelled by
{\mathbb{Z}}
in an ascending order going from left to right, by putting a bead at the position
a
for each
a\in\mathsf{B}
.
To obtain the
e
-abacus display of
\mathsf{B}
, we cut its
\infty
-abacus display into sections
\{ae,ae+1,\dotsc,ae+e-1\}
(
a\in{\mathbb{Z}}
), and put the section
\{ae,ae+1,\dotsc,ae+e-1\}
directly on top of
\{(a+1)e,(a+1)e+1,\dotsc,(a+1)e+e-1\}
.
Thus the
e
-abacus have
e
infinitely long vertical runners, which we label as
0,1,\dotsc,e-1
going from left to right, and countably infinitely many horizontal rows, labelled by
{\mathbb{Z}}
in an ascending order going from top to bottom, and the position on row
a
and runner
i
equals
ae+i
.
The
e
-quotient of
\mathsf{B}
, denoted
\mathsf{quot}_{e}(\mathsf{B})=(\mathsf{B}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{B}_{e})
, can be obtained as follows: for each
i\in\mathbb{Z}/e\mathbb{Z}
, treat each runner
i
as an
\infty
-abacus, with the position
ae+i
relabelled as
a
, and obtain the subset
\mathsf{B}_{i+1}
from the beads in runner
i
of the
e
-abacus of
\mathsf{B}
. Formally,

\mathsf{B}_{i+1}=\{a\in{\mathbb{Z}}\mid ae+i\in\mathsf{B}\}=\{\tfrac{x-i}{e}\mid x\in\mathsf{B},\ x\equiv_{e}i\}.

Its
e
-core
, denoted
\mathsf{core}_{e}(\mathsf{B})
, can be obtained by repeatedly sliding its beads in its
e
-abacus up their respectively runners to fill up the vacant positions above them, and its
e
-weight
, denoted
\mathsf{wt}_{e}(\mathsf{B})
, is the total number of times the beads move one position up their runners.
Note that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{e}\mathcal{s}(\mathsf{B}_{i})=\mathcal{s}(\mathsf{B})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{s}(\mathsf{core}_{e}(\mathsf{B})).

Given
\beta
-set
\mathsf{B}
and
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, define

\mathsf{B}^{+k}:=\{x+k\mid x\in\mathsf{B}\}.

Then
\mathsf{B}^{+k}
is a
\beta
-set with
\mathcal{s}(\mathsf{B}^{+k})=\mathcal{s}(\mathsf{B})+k
. Furthermore, if
\mathsf{quot}_{e}(\mathsf{B})=(\mathsf{B}_{1},\mathsf{B}_{2},\dotsc,\mathsf{B}_{e})
, then
\mathsf{quot}_{e}(\mathsf{B}^{+1})=((\mathsf{B}_{e})^{+1},\mathsf{B}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{B}_{e-1})
.
Consequently,

\mathsf{quot}_{e}(\mathsf{B}^{+ke})=((\mathsf{B}_{1})^{+k},(\mathsf{B}_{2})^{+k},\dotsc,(\mathsf{B}_{e})^{+k}).

(2.1)

In addition, we also have

\displaystyle\mathsf{core}_{e}(\mathsf{B}^{+k})

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{core}_{e}(\mathsf{B}))^{+k};

\displaystyle\mathsf{wt}_{e}(\mathsf{B}^{+k})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{wt}_{e}(\mathsf{B}).

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
\mathsf{B}
and
\mathsf{C}
be
\beta
-sets.
Then

\mathcal{s}(\mathsf{B})-\mathcal{s}(\mathsf{C})=|\mathsf{B}\setminus\mathsf{C}|-|\mathsf{C}\setminus\mathsf{B}|.

In particular:

(1)

if
k:=\mathcal{s}(\mathsf{B})-\mathcal{s}(\mathsf{C})>0
, then there exist distinct
b_{1},\dotsc,b_{k}\in\mathsf{B}\setminus\mathsf{C}
;

(2)

if
\mathsf{B}=\mathbb{Z}_{<m}\cup S
for some
m\in\mathbb{Z}
with
S\cap\mathbb{Z}_{<m}=\emptyset
, then
\mathcal{s}(\mathsf{B})=m+|S|
.

Proof.

This is
[
LT24
, Lemma 2.2]
. Part (1) then follows since
|\mathsf{B}\setminus\mathsf{C}|\geq\mathcal{s}(\mathsf{B})-\mathcal{s}(\mathsf{C})
, and part (2) follows by putting
\mathsf{C}=\mathbb{Z}_{<m}
.
∎

For each
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}/e{\mathbb{Z}}
and
\mathsf{B}\in\mathcal{B}
, define
\mathsf{hub}_{i}(\mathsf{B})=\mathsf{hub}_{i,e}(\mathsf{B})
by

\displaystyle\mathsf{hub}_{i}(\mathsf{B})

\displaystyle:=|\{x\in\mathsf{B}\mid x\equiv_{e}i,\,x-1\notin\mathsf{B}\}|-|\{x\in\mathsf{B}\mid x\equiv_{e}i-1,\,x+1\notin\mathsf{B}\}|

Furthermore, call the
e
-tuple
(\mathsf{hub}_{0}(\mathsf{B}),\dotsc,\mathsf{hub}_{e-1}(\mathsf{B}))
the
e
-hub of
\mathsf{B}
, denoted
\mathsf{Hub}_{e}(\mathsf{B})
.

We can deduce from Lemma
2.1
that if
\mathsf{quot}_{e}(\mathsf{B})=(\mathsf{B}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{B}_{e})
, then

\mathsf{hub}_{j}(\mathsf{B})=\mathcal{s}(\mathsf{B}_{j+1})-\mathcal{s}(\mathsf{B}_{j})-\delta_{j0},

(2.2)

for all
j\in\mathbb{Z}/e\mathbb{Z}
,
where

\mathsf{B}_{0}
is to be read as
\mathsf{B}_{e}
, and

\delta_{i0}
equals
1
when
i=0
, and
0
otherwise.
In particular,

\mathsf{Hub}_{e}(\mathsf{B})=\mathsf{Hub}_{e}(\mathsf{core}_{e}(\mathsf{B})).

(2.3)

Definition 2.2
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
. Define a function
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{s}}=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{s}}_{m}:\mathcal{B}^{m}\to\mathbb{Z}^{m}
by

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{s}}(\mathsf{B}^{(1)},\dotsc,\mathsf{B}^{(m)})=(\mathcal{s}(\mathsf{B}^{(1)}),\dotsc,\mathcal{s}(\mathsf{B}^{(m)})).

2.3.
Partitions

A partition is a weakly decreasing infinite sequence of nonnegative integers that is eventually zero. Given partition
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\dotsc)
, we write

\displaystyle\mathcal{l}(\lambda)

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{max}\{a\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}:\lambda_{a}>0\};

\displaystyle|\lambda|

\displaystyle:=\sum_{a=1}^{\mathcal{l}(\lambda)}\lambda_{a}.

When
|\lambda|=n
, we say that
\lambda
is a partition of
n
.
Its Young diagram
[\lambda]
is defined as

[\lambda]=\{(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}\mid 1\leq i\leq\mathcal{l}(\lambda),1\leq j\leq\lambda_{i}\}.

We write
\mathcal{P}(n)
for the set of partitions of
n
and
\mathcal{P}
for the set of all partitions. The unique partition of
0
, also known as the empty partition, shall be denoted as
\varnothing
.

Let
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\dotsc)\in\mathcal{P}
. For each
t\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\beta_{t}(\lambda):=\{\lambda_{i}+t-i\mid i\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}\}

is a
\beta
-set, with
\mathcal{s}(\beta_{t}(\lambda))=t
. We call
\beta_{t}(\lambda)
the
\beta
-set of
\lambda
with charge
t
.
The map
\beta:\mathcal{P}\times\mathbb{Z}\to\mathcal{B}
, defined by
\beta(\lambda,t)=\beta_{t}(\lambda)
, is a bijection, and for each
t\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\beta_{t}=\beta(-,t)
gives a bijection between
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{B}(t)
. In particular, when
\mathsf{B}
is a
\beta
-set, we shall identify
\beta^{-1}(\mathsf{B})
with the unique partition
\lambda
satisfying
\beta(\lambda,\mathcal{s}(\mathsf{B}))=\beta_{\mathcal{s}(\mathsf{B})}(\lambda)=\mathsf{B}
.

The
e
-weight
\mathsf{wt}_{e}(\lambda)
and the
e
-core
\mathsf{core}_{e}(\lambda)
of
\lambda
can be unambiguously defined to be
\mathsf{wt}_{e}(\beta_{t}(\lambda))
and
\beta^{-1}(\mathsf{core}_{e}(\beta_{t}(\lambda)))
respectively for any
t\in\mathbb{Z}
.

We note that if
\mathsf{B}
is a
\beta
-set with
\mathsf{quot}_{e}(\mathsf{B})=(\mathsf{B}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{B}_{e})
, then
\mathsf{core}_{e}(\mathsf{B})
is the unique
\beta
-set whose
e
-quotient is
(\beta_{\mathcal{s}(\mathsf{B}_{1})}(\varnothing),\dotsc,\beta_{\mathcal{s}(\mathsf{B}_{e})}(\varnothing))
and
\mathsf{wt}_{e}(\mathsf{B})=\sum_{i=1}^{e}|\beta^{-1}(\mathsf{B}_{i})|
.

2.4.
Multipartitions

An
\ell
-partition of
n
is an element
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=(\lambda^{(1)},\dotsc,\lambda^{(\ell)})\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}|\lambda^{(i)}|=n
.
We write
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}(n)
for the set of all
\ell
-partitions of
n
.
The unique
\ell
-partition of
0
shall be denoted
\boldsymbol{\varnothing}
. Of course,
\boldsymbol{\varnothing}
depends on
\ell
, which will always be clear in the context in this paper.

Let
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
. The
Young diagram of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
is

[\boldsymbol{\lambda}]=\{(a,b,i)\in({\mathbb{Z}}^{+})^{3}\mid a\leq\mathcal{l}(\lambda^{(i)}),\ b\leq\lambda^{(j)}_{a},\ i\leq\ell\}.

Elements of
[\boldsymbol{\lambda}]
are called nodes.

We usually associate
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
with an
\ell
-charge

\mathbf{t}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
, and we write
(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})
for such a pair. The set of such pairs is then naturally identified with
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}\times{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
.

Let
\mathbf{t}=(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{\ell})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
.
For
(a,b,i)\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}
with
i\in[1,\,\ell]
, define its
\mathbf{t}
-content
, denoted
\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(a,b,i)
, to be
b-a+t_{i}
.
Given
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
, the rightmost nodes of
[\boldsymbol{\lambda}]
are of the form
(a,\lambda^{(i)}_{a},i)
for
a\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
and
i\in[1,\,\ell]
.
Note that

\beta_{t_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)})=\{\lambda^{(i)}_{a}-a+t_{i}\mid a\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}\}=\{\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(a,\lambda^{(i)}_{a},i)\mid a\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}\},

so that
\beta_{t_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)})
is the set of
\mathbf{t}
-contents of the rightmost nodes of
[\boldsymbol{\lambda}]
coming from
\lambda^{(i)}
.

Given
(a,b,i)\in[\boldsymbol{\lambda}]
, its
(e,\mathbf{t})
-residue
, denoted
\mathsf{res}_{e}^{\mathbf{t}}(a,b,i)
,
is the residue class of its
\mathbf{t}
-content modulo
e
; i.e.
\mathsf{res}_{e}^{\mathbf{t}}(a,b,i)\in\mathbb{Z}/e\mathbb{Z}
with
\mathsf{res}_{e}^{\mathbf{t}}(a,b,i)\equiv_{e}\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(a,b,i)
.
The
(e,\mathbf{t})
-residues of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
, denoted
\mathsf{res}^{\mathbf{t}}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
, is the multiset consisting precisely of
\mathsf{res}_{e}^{\mathbf{t}}(a,b,i)
for all
(a,b,i)\in[\boldsymbol{\lambda}]
.

If removing a node
\mathfrak{n}
of
[\boldsymbol{\lambda}]
from
[\boldsymbol{\lambda}]
yields a Young diagram
[\boldsymbol{\mu}]
for some
\ell
-partition
\boldsymbol{\mu}
, we call
\mathfrak{n}

a removable node of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
and
an addable node of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
, and write
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{-\mathfrak{n}}:=\boldsymbol{\mu}
and
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{+\mathfrak{n}}:=\boldsymbol{\lambda}
.
If
(a,b,i)
is a removable node of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
, then
b=\lambda^{(i)}_{a}>\lambda^{(i)}_{a+1}
, and so

\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(a,\lambda_{a}^{(i)},i)=\lambda_{a}^{(i)}-a+t_{i}>\lambda_{(a+1)}^{(i)}-a+t_{i}>\lambda_{(a+1)}^{(i)}-(a+1)+t_{i}=\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(a+1,\lambda^{(i)}_{a+1},i).

Thus such a removable node corresponds bijectively to an
x\in\beta_{t_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)})
with
x-1\notin\beta_{t_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)})
(namely,
x=\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(a,b,i)
).
Note that if
\mathsf{res}_{e}^{\mathbf{t}}(a,b,i)=j
, then
x=\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(a,b,i)\equiv_{e}\mathsf{res}^{\mathbf{t}}_{e}(a,b,i)=j
, so that
x
lies in runner
j
of the abacus display of
\beta_{t_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)})
.
In the same way, an addable node
(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},i^{\prime})
of
[\boldsymbol{\lambda}]
corresponds bijectvely to an
x
such that
x-1\in\beta_{i^{\prime}}(\lambda^{(i^{\prime})})
but
x\notin\beta_{i^{\prime}}(\lambda^{(i^{\prime})})
(namely,
x=\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},i^{\prime})
).
In particular, this shows that each
\lambda^{(i)}
can contribute at most one removable node and at most one addable node, and not both, with a given
\mathbf{t}
-content.

We denote the set of addable (resp. removable) nodes of
[\boldsymbol{\lambda}]
by
\mathsf{A}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
(resp.
\mathsf{R}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
), and its subset consisting of nodes with
(e,\mathbf{t})
-residue
j
(
j\in\mathbb{Z}/e\mathbb{Z}
) by
\mathsf{A}^{e,\mathbf{t}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
(resp.
\mathsf{R}^{e,\mathbf{t}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
).
Furthermore, write
\mathsf{AR}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}):=\mathsf{A}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\cup\mathsf{R}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
and

\mathsf{AR}^{e,\mathbf{t}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}):=\mathsf{A}^{e,\mathbf{t}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\cup\mathsf{R}^{e,\mathbf{t}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
.

For
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=(\lambda^{(1)},\dotsc,\lambda^{(\ell)})\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
and
\mathbf{t}=(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{\ell})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
, we define

\mathsf{hub}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t}):=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathsf{hub}_{j}(\beta_{t_{j}}(\lambda^{(i)}))

for each
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}/e{\mathbb{Z}}
.
This is the number of removable nodes of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
with
(e,\mathbf{t})
-residue
j
minus the number of addable nodes of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
with
(e,\mathbf{t})
-residue
j
.
The
e
-hub of
(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})
, denoted
\mathsf{Hub}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})
, is then

\mathsf{Hub}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t}):=(\mathsf{hub}_{0}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t}),\dotsc,\mathsf{hub}_{e-1}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})).

For convenience, for
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=(\lambda^{(1)},\dotsc,\lambda^{(\ell)})\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
and
\mathbf{t}=(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{\ell})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
, we shall use the shorthand

\beta_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}):=(\beta_{t_{1}}(\lambda^{(1)}),\dotsc,\beta_{t_{\ell}}(\lambda^{(\ell)}))\in\mathcal{B}^{\ell}.

2.5.
Uglov’s map

For each
i\in\{1,\dotsc,\ell\}
, define
\upsilon_{i}\ (=\upsilon_{e,\ell,i}):\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}
by

\upsilon_{i}(x)=(x-\bar{x})\ell+(\ell-i)e+\bar{x}.

Here
\bar{x}
is defined by
x\equiv_{e}\bar{x}\in\{0,1,\dotsc,e-1\}
. In other words, if
x=ae+b
, where
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
with
0\leq b<e
, then

\upsilon_{i}(x)=ae\ell+(\ell-i)e+b.

We record some properties of these
\upsilon_{i}
’s which can be easily verified.

Lemma 2.3
.

(1)

\upsilon_{i}(x)\equiv_{e}x
.

(2)

\upsilon_{i}
is injective.

(3)

\mathbb{Z}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}\upsilon_{i}(\mathbb{Z})
(disjoint union).

(4)

\upsilon_{i}(x)-e=\upsilon_{i+1}(x)
for all
i\in[1,\,\ell-1]
, and
\upsilon_{\ell}(x)-e=\upsilon_{1}(x-e)
.

In
[
Ugl00
]
, Uglov defines a map
\mathbf{U}\ (=\mathbf{U}_{e,\ell}):\mathcal{B}^{\ell}\to\mathcal{B}
as follows:

\mathbf{U}(\mathsf{B}^{(1)},\dotsc,\mathsf{B}^{(\ell)}):=\bigcup_{j=1}^{\ell}\upsilon_{j}(\mathsf{B}^{(j)})\qquad(\mathsf{B}^{(1)},\dotsc,\mathsf{B}^{(\ell)}\in\mathcal{B}).

The best way to understand
\mathbf{U}
is via the abaci.
First we stack
\infty
-abacus displays of
\mathsf{B}^{(j)}
’s on top of each other, with the display of
\mathsf{B}^{(1)}
at the bottom and that of
\mathsf{B}^{(\ell)}
at the top.
Next, we cut up this stacked
\infty
-abaci into sections with positions
\{ae,ae+1,\dotsc,ae+e-1\}
(
a\in{\mathbb{Z}}
), and putting the section with positions
\{ae,ae+1,\dotsc,ae+e-1\}
on top of that with positions
\{(a+1)e,(a+1)e+1,\dotsc,(a+1)e+e-1\}
(thus the section of the
\infty
-abacus of
\mathsf{B}^{(1)}
with positions
\{ae,ae+1,\dotsc,ae+e-1\}
is placed directly on top of the section of the
\infty
-abacus of
\mathsf{B}^{(\ell)}
with positions
\{(a+1)e,(a+1)e+1,\dotsc,(a+1)e+e-1\}
), and relabel the rows of this
e
-abacus by
{\mathbb{Z}}
in ascending order, with
0
labelling the section of the
\infty
-abacus of
\mathsf{B}^{(\ell)}
with positions
\{0,1,\dotsc,e-1\}
.
This yields the
e
-abacus display of
\mathbf{U}(\mathsf{B}^{(1)},\dotsc,\mathsf{B}^{(\ell)})
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=(\lambda^{(1)},\dotsc,\lambda^{(\ell)})\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
and
\mathbf{t}=(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{\ell})\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
.
Recall that
|\mathbf{t}|=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}t_{i}
and

\beta_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})=(\beta_{t_{1}}(\lambda^{(1)}),\dotsc,\beta_{t_{\ell}}(\lambda^{(\ell)}))
.
It is not difficult to see from the description of
\mathbf{U}
that the addable nodes and removable nodes of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
with nonzero
(e,\mathbf{t})
-residue correspond bijectively to the addable nodes and removable nodes of
\beta^{-1}(\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})))
with nonzero
(e,|\mathbf{t}|)
-residue.
We record this formally below.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
, and for all
\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
, let
\Phi_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\nu}):=\beta^{-1}(\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\nu})))
.
For
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}

and
j\in[1,\,e-1]
,
there is a unique function
\phi:\mathsf{AR}^{e,\mathbf{t}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\to\mathsf{AR}^{e,|\mathbf{t}|}_{j}(\Phi_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))
satisfying

\upsilon_{i}(\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathfrak{n}))=\mathsf{cont}_{|\mathbf{t}|}(\phi(\mathfrak{n}))

for all
\mathfrak{n}=(a,b,i)\in\mathsf{AR}^{e,\mathbf{t}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
.
Furthermore:

(1)

\phi
sends removable (resp. addable) nodes of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
to removable (resp. addable) nodes of
\Phi_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
;

(2)

\phi
is bijective;

(3)

\Phi_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{+\mathfrak{n}})=(\Phi_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))^{+\phi(\mathfrak{n})}
if
\mathfrak{n}
is addable and

\Phi_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{-\mathfrak{n}})=(\Phi_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))^{-\phi(\mathfrak{n})}
if
\mathfrak{n}
is removable.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{t}=(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{\ell})
and
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=(\lambda^{(1)},\dotsc,\lambda^{(\ell)})\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
.
For each
\mathfrak{n}=(a,b,i)\in\mathsf{AR}^{e,\mathbf{t}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
,
exactly one of
\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathfrak{n})
and
\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathfrak{n})-1
lies in
\beta_{t_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)})
,
and hence
exactly one of
\upsilon_{i}(\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathfrak{n}))
and
\upsilon_{i}(\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathfrak{n})-1)
lies in

\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))=\beta_{|\mathbf{t}|}(\Phi_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))
.
Now,

\upsilon_{i}(\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathfrak{n}))\equiv_{e}\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathfrak{n})\equiv_{e}\mathsf{res}^{\mathbf{t}}_{e}(\mathfrak{n})\equiv_{e}j\neq 0

by Lemma
2.3
(1), so that

\upsilon_{i}(\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathfrak{n})-1)=\upsilon_{i}(\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathfrak{n}))-1
.
Thus
\upsilon_{i}(\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathfrak{n}))=\mathsf{cont}_{|\mathbf{t}|}(\mathfrak{m}_{\mathfrak{n}})
for a unique
\mathfrak{m}_{\mathfrak{n}}\in\mathsf{AR}(\Phi_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))
, with

\mathsf{res}^{|\mathbf{t}|}_{e}(\mathfrak{m}_{\mathfrak{n}})\equiv_{e}\mathsf{cont}_{|\mathbf{t}|}(\mathfrak{m}_{\mathfrak{n}})=\upsilon_{i}(\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathfrak{n}))\equiv_{e}j.

Thus, we have a function
\phi:\mathsf{AR}^{e,\mathbf{t}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\to\mathsf{AR}^{e,|\mathbf{t}|}_{j}(\Phi_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))
, defined by
\phi(\mathfrak{n})=\mathfrak{m}_{\mathfrak{n}}
, satisfying

\upsilon_{i}(\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathfrak{n}))=\mathsf{cont}_{|\mathbf{t}|}(\mathfrak{m}_{\mathfrak{n}})=\mathsf{cont}_{|\mathbf{t}|}(\phi(\mathfrak{n})).

Note that the uniqueness of
\mathfrak{m}_{\mathfrak{n}}
ensures that
f
is both well-defined and unique satisfying
\upsilon_{i}(\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathfrak{n}))=\mathsf{cont}_{|\mathbf{t}|}(\phi(\mathfrak{n}))
.
Furthermore,

\displaystyle\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathfrak{n})\in\beta_{t_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)})\ \Leftrightarrow\ \mathsf{cont}_{|\mathbf{t}|}(\phi(\mathfrak{n}))=\upsilon_{i}(\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathfrak{n}))\in\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))=\beta_{|\mathbf{t}|}(\Phi_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))

so that
\mathfrak{n}
is removable if and only if
\phi(\mathfrak{n})
is removable.

If
\mathfrak{n}=(a,b,i),\mathfrak{n^{\prime}}=(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},i^{\prime})\in\mathsf{AR}^{e,\mathbf{t}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
are such that
\phi(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})=\phi(\mathfrak{n})
, then

\upsilon_{i^{\prime}}(\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}))=\mathsf{cont}_{|\mathbf{t}|}(\phi(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}))=\mathsf{cont}_{|\mathbf{t}|}(\phi(\mathfrak{n}))=\upsilon_{i}(\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathfrak{n}))

so that
i^{\prime}=i
since the
\upsilon_{a}(\mathbb{Z})
’s are pairwise disjoint,
and hence
\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})=\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathfrak{n})
since
\upsilon_{i}
is injective (see Lemma
2.3
(2,3)).
This further implies that
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{n}
as

\lambda^{(i)}
has at most one removable node and at most one addable node, but not both, with a given content.
Thus
f
is injective.

If
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathsf{AR}^{e,|\mathbf{t}|}_{j}(\Phi_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))
, then exactly one of
\mathsf{cont}_{|\mathbf{t}|}(\mathfrak{m})
or
\mathsf{cont}_{|\mathbf{t}|}(\mathfrak{m})-1
lies in

\beta_{|\mathbf{t}|}(\Phi_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))=\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}\upsilon_{i}(\beta_{t_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)})),

and hence in

\upsilon_{i}(\beta_{t_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)}))
for a unique
i
(since the
\upsilon_{a}(\mathbb{Z})
’s are pairwise disjoint).
Thus exactly one of
\upsilon_{i}^{-1}(\mathsf{cont}_{|\mathbf{t}|}(\mathfrak{m}))
or
\upsilon_{i}^{-1}(\mathsf{cont}_{|\mathbf{t}|}(\mathfrak{m})-1)
lies in
\beta_{t_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)})
.
Since

\displaystyle\upsilon_{i}^{-1}(\mathsf{cont}_{|\mathbf{t}|}(\mathfrak{m}))

\displaystyle\equiv_{e}\mathsf{cont}_{|\mathbf{t}|}(\mathfrak{m})\equiv_{e}\mathsf{res}^{|\mathbf{t}|}_{e}(\mathfrak{m})=j\neq 0,

we have
\upsilon_{i}^{-1}(\mathsf{cont}_{|\mathbf{t}|}(\mathfrak{m})-1)=\upsilon_{i}^{-1}(\mathsf{cont}_{|\mathbf{t}|}(\mathfrak{m}))-1
.
Thus
\upsilon_{i}^{-1}(\mathsf{cont}_{|\mathbf{t}|}(\mathfrak{m}))=\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathfrak{n})
for some
\mathfrak{n}=(a,b,i)\in\mathsf{AR}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
.
Now

\displaystyle\mathsf{res}^{\mathbf{t}}_{e}(\mathfrak{n})\equiv_{e}\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathfrak{n})

\displaystyle=\upsilon_{i}^{-1}(\mathsf{cont}_{|\mathbf{t}|}(\mathfrak{m}))\equiv_{e}j.

Thus
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathsf{AR}^{e,\mathbf{t}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
, and

\mathsf{cont}_{|\mathbf{t}|}(\mathfrak{m})=\upsilon_{i}(\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathfrak{n}))
,
so that
\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{m}_{\mathfrak{n}}=\phi(\mathfrak{n})
.
Thus
f
is surjective.

Let
\mathfrak{n}=(a,b,i)\in\mathsf{AR}^{e,\mathbf{t}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
. If
\mathfrak{n}
is addable, then

\beta_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{+\mathfrak{n}})=(\beta_{t_{1}}(\lambda^{(1)}),\dotsc,\beta_{t_{i-1}}(\lambda^{(i-1)}),\beta_{t_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)})\cup\{\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathfrak{n})\}\setminus\{\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathfrak{n})-1\},\beta_{t_{i+1}}(\lambda^{(i+1)}),\dotsc,\beta_{t_{\ell}}(\lambda^{(\ell)})),

so that

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{+\mathfrak{n}}))

\displaystyle=\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))\cup\{\upsilon_{i}(\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathfrak{n}))\}\setminus\{\upsilon_{i}(\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathfrak{n}))-1\}

\displaystyle=\beta_{|\mathbf{t}|}(\Phi_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))\cup\{\mathsf{cont}_{|\mathbf{t}|}(\phi(\mathfrak{n}))\}\setminus\{\mathsf{cont}_{|\mathbf{t}|}(\phi(\mathfrak{n}))-1\}=\beta_{|\mathbf{t}|}((\Phi_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))^{+\phi(\mathfrak{n})}),

so that
\Phi_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{+\mathfrak{n}})=(\Phi_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))^{+\phi(\mathfrak{n})}
.
Similarly, one also gets
\Phi_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{-\mathfrak{n}})=(\Phi_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))^{-\phi(\mathfrak{n})}
if
\mathfrak{n}
is removable.
∎

For each
(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}\times\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
, define

\mathsf{core}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t}):=\mathsf{core}_{e}(\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})))\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathsf{wt}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})=\mathsf{wt}_{e}(\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))).

Note that while our
\mathsf{wt}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})
is the same as that in
[
LT24
]
, our
\mathsf{core}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})
is defined slightly differently—here
\mathsf{core}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})
is a
\beta
-set, while
\mathsf{core}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})
in
[
LT24
]
is defined to be the associated partition.

2.6.
The extended affine Weyl group
\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{m}

Let
m\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}
. The symmetric group on
m
letters is denoted by
\mathfrak{S}_{m}
. We view
\mathfrak{S}_{m}
as a group of functions acting on
\{1,2,\dotsc,m\}
so that we compose from right to left; for example,
(1,2)(2,3)=(1,2,3)
. This is a Coxeter group of type
A_{m-1}
, with Coxeter generators
\mathsf{s}_{i}=(i,i+1)
for
1\leq i\leq m-1
.

Given any nonempty set
X
,
\mathfrak{S}_{m}
has a natural right place permutation action on
X^{m}
via

(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{m})^{\sigma}=(x_{\sigma(1)},\dotsc,x_{\sigma(m)}).

Let
\{\mathbf{e}_{1},\dotsc,\mathbf{e}_{m}\}
denote the standard
{\mathbb{Z}}
-basis for the free (left)
{\mathbb{Z}}
-module
{\mathbb{Z}}^{m}
.
The right place permutation action of
\mathfrak{S}_{m}
on
{\mathbb{Z}}^{m}
induces the semidirect product
\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{m}=\mathfrak{S}_{m}\ltimes{\mathbb{Z}}^{m}
,
where

(\sigma\mathbf{t})(\tau\mathbf{u})=(\sigma\tau)(\mathbf{t}^{\tau}+\mathbf{u})=(\sigma\tau)(t_{\tau(1)}+u_{1},\dotsc,t_{\tau(m)}+u_{m})

(2.4)

for all
\sigma,\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{m}
and
\mathbf{t},\mathbf{u}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{m}
with
\mathbf{t}=(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{m})
and
\mathbf{u}=(u_{1},\dotsc,u_{m})
.
This is an extended affine Weyl group of type
A^{(1)}_{m-1}
, and it contains the affine Weyl group
\mathbf{W}_{m}
of type
\widetilde{A}_{m-1}
generated by
\{\mathsf{s}_{0},\mathsf{s}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{s}_{m-1}\}
, where
\mathsf{s}_{a}=(a,a+1)\in\mathfrak{S}_{m}
for
1\leq a\leq m-1
as before, and
\mathsf{s}_{0}=(\mathbf{e}_{1}-\mathbf{e}_{m})(1,m)
.
Note that under this realisation,

\mathbf{W}_{m}=\{\sigma\mathbf{t}\in\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{m}\mid\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{m},\ \mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m},\ |\mathbf{t}|=0\}.

(2.5)

It is sometimes convenient to write an element of
\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{m}
in the form
\mathbf{t}\sigma
where
\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
and
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{m}
, which is equal to
\sigma\mathbf{t}^{\sigma}
.

Given
w=\sigma\mathbf{t}\in\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{m}
where
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{m}
and
\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
, we write
\overline{w}
for
\sigma
(thus
\overline{w}
denotes the projection of
w
onto
\mathfrak{S}_{m}
).

We have a right action of
\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{\ell}
on
{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
via

\displaystyle\mathbf{t}^{\sigma\mathbf{u}}=\mathbf{t}^{\sigma}+e\mathbf{u}\quad\text{and}\quad\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\sigma\mathbf{u}}=\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\sigma}

for
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
,
\mathbf{t},\mathbf{u}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
and
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{\ell}
, which further induces a right action on
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}\times{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
.
We write
x^{\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{\ell}}
for the orbit of
x
(for
x
in
{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
or
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
or
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}\times{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
), and note that
\mathsf{res}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})
and
\mathsf{Hub}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})
are invariant under this action; i.e.

\mathsf{res}^{\mathbf{t}^{w}}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{w})=\mathsf{res}^{\mathbf{t}}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{Hub}_{e}((\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})^{w})=\mathsf{Hub}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})

for all
w\in\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{\ell}
.

Let

\displaystyle\mathcal{A_{\mathsf{F}}}

\displaystyle:=\{(a_{1},\dotsc,a_{\ell})\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}\mid 0\leq a_{1}\leq a_{2}\leq\dotsb\leq a_{\ell}<e\};

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{e}^{\ell}

\displaystyle:=\{(a_{1},\dotsc,a_{\ell})\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}\mid a_{1}\leq a_{2}\leq\dotsb\leq a_{\ell}<a_{1}+e\};

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{e}^{\ell}

\displaystyle:=\{(a_{1},\dotsc,a_{\ell})\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}\mid a_{1}\leq a_{2}\leq\dotsb\leq a_{\ell}\leq a_{1}+e\}.

Clearly,
\mathcal{A_{\mathsf{F}}}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{e}^{\ell}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{e}^{\ell}
, and
\mathcal{A_{\mathsf{F}}}
is a fundamental domain of the action of
\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{\ell}
on
\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}\times\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
. If
\mathsf{hub}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})\leq 0
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}/e\mathbb{Z}
, then

\mathsf{core}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})=\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\varnothing}))
for a unique
\mathbf{t}^{*}\in\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{e}^{\ell}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathsf{quot}_{e}(\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})))=(\mathsf{C}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{C}_{e})
.
Then

\mathcal{s}(\mathsf{C}_{j+1})-\mathcal{s}(\mathsf{C}_{j})-\delta_{j0}=\mathsf{hub}_{j}(\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})))=\mathsf{hub}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})+(\ell-1)\delta_{j0}

for all
j\in\mathbb{Z}/e\mathbb{Z}

by (
2.2
) and
[
LT24
, Lemma 2.6(3)]
, where
\mathsf{C}_{0}
is to be read as
\mathsf{C}_{e}
.
Thus

\mathcal{s}(\mathsf{C}_{1})\geq\mathcal{s}(\mathsf{C}_{2})\geq\dotsb\geq\mathcal{s}(\mathsf{C}_{e})\geq\mathcal{s}(\mathsf{C}_{1})-\ell.

Consequently, since
\mathsf{quot}_{e}(\mathsf{core}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t}))=\beta_{\mathbf{s}}(\boldsymbol{\varnothing})
where
\mathbf{s}=(\mathcal{s}(\mathsf{C}_{1}),\dotsc,\mathcal{s}(\mathsf{C}_{e}))
, this shows that for each
x\in\mathsf{core}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})
with
x\equiv_{e}j\in\mathbb{Z}/e\mathbb{Z}
, we have
x-1-\delta_{j0}\ell\in\mathsf{core}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})
. Thus
\mathsf{core}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})=\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\varnothing}))
for some unique
\mathbf{t}^{*}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
.
That
\mathbf{t}^{*}\in\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{e}^{\ell}
follows from
[
JL21
, Proposition 2.14]
.
∎

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
, and let
\rho_{\ell}=(1,2,\dotsc,\ell)\in\mathfrak{S}_{\ell}
.

(1)

For
a\in\mathbb{Z}
and
b\in\mathbb{Z}/\ell\mathbb{Z}
,
(\rho_{\ell}\mathbf{e}_{\ell})^{a\ell+b}=\rho_{\ell}^{b}\mathbf{v}_{\ell}(a\ell+b)
(recall from Subsection
2.1
that

\mathbf{v}_{\ell}(a\ell+b)=(a,\dotsc,a,\overbrace{a+1,\dotsc,a+1}^{b\text{ times}})\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}).

In particular,
\rho_{\ell}\mathbf{e}_{\ell}
has infinite order as an element of
\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{\ell}
.

(2)

We have
\mathbf{t}\in\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{e}^{\ell}
if and only if
\mathbf{t}^{\rho_{\ell}\mathbf{e}_{\ell}}\in\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{e}^{\ell}
.

(3)

If
\mathbf{t}\in\mathcal{A}_{e}^{\ell}
and
\mathbf{t}^{\sigma\mathbf{v}}\in\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{e}^{\ell}
for some
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{\ell}
and
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
, then
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{v}_{\ell}(a\ell+b)
and
\sigma\rho_{\ell}^{-b}\in\left<\mathsf{s}_{i}\mid t_{i}=t_{i+1}\right>
for some
a\in\mathbb{Z}
and
b\in\mathbb{Z}/\ell\mathbb{Z}
.

(4)

For
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
,

\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}^{\rho_{\ell}\mathbf{e}_{\ell}}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\rho_{\ell}\mathbf{e}_{\ell}}))=(\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})))^{+e}.

(5)

Restricting the right action of
\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{\ell}
on
\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
to
\left<\rho_{\ell}\mathbf{e}_{\ell}\right>
, we get a transitive and faithful action of
\left<\rho_{\ell}\mathbf{e}_{\ell}\right>
on
\mathbf{t}^{\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{\ell}}\cap\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{e}^{\ell}
.

In particular, if
w,w^{\prime}\in\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{\ell}
such that
\mathbf{t}^{w},\mathbf{t}^{w^{\prime}}\in\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{e}^{\ell}
,
then
\mathbf{t}^{w^{\prime}}=(\mathbf{t}^{w})^{(\rho_{\ell}\mathbf{e}_{\ell})^{k}}
for a unique
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

Parts (1)–(3) is
[
LT24
, Proposition 3.5]
, while part (4) follows from
[
LT24
, Proposition 3.3(1)]
.

For part (5), that
\left<\rho_{\ell}\mathbf{e}_{\ell}\right>
acts on
\mathbf{t}^{\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{\ell}}\cap\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{e}^{\ell}
follows from part (3).
Let
\mathbf{t}_{\mathsf{F}}=(u_{1},\dotsc,u_{\ell})\in\mathbf{t}^{\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{\ell}}\cap\mathcal{A_{\mathsf{F}}}
, and let
w_{0}\in\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{\ell}
be such that
\mathbf{t}_{\mathsf{F}}=\mathbf{t}^{w_{0}}
.
To show that the action of
\left<\rho_{\ell}\mathbf{e}_{\ell}\right>
on
\mathbf{t}^{\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{\ell}}\cap\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{e}^{\ell}
is transitive, let
w\in\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{\ell}
be such that
\mathbf{t}^{w}\in\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{e}^{\ell}
.
By part (4), since
\mathbf{t}_{\mathsf{F}}\in\mathcal{A}_{e}^{\ell}
and
\mathbf{t}^{w}=(\mathbf{t}^{w_{0}})^{w_{0}^{-1}w}=\mathbf{t}_{\mathsf{F}}^{w_{0}^{-1}w}\in\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{e}^{\ell}
, we have that

w_{0}^{-1}w=\sigma\mathbf{v}_{\ell}(a\ell+b)
for some
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{\ell}
,
a\in\mathbb{Z}
and
b\in\mathbb{Z}/\ell\mathbb{Z}
such that
\sigma\rho_{\ell}^{-b}\in\left<\mathsf{s}_{i}\mid u_{i}=u_{i+1}\right>
.
Consequently,

\mathbf{t}^{w}=\mathbf{t}_{\mathsf{F}}^{w_{0}^{-1}w}=(\mathbf{t}_{\mathsf{F}}^{\sigma\rho_{\ell}^{-b}})^{\rho_{\ell}^{b}\mathbf{v}_{\ell}(a\ell+b)}=\mathbf{t}_{\mathsf{F}}^{(\rho_{\ell}\mathbf{e}_{\ell})^{a\ell+b}},

as desired.
That the action is faithful now follows immediately from the fact that
\mathbf{t}^{\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{\ell}}\cap\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{e}^{\ell}
is an infinite set.
∎

The
e
-cores and
e
-weights of
\ell
-partitions with their associated charges in the same
\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{\ell}
-orbit are related as follows:

Theorem 2.7

(
[
LT24
, Theorem 3.6]
)
.

Let
(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}\times\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
, and let
(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{u})\in(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})^{\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{\ell}}
. Then

(1)

\beta^{-1}(\mathsf{core}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{u}))=\beta^{-1}(\mathsf{core}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t}))
;

(2)

\mathsf{wt}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{u})=\mathrm{min}(\mathsf{wt}_{e}((\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})^{\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{\ell}}))
if and only if
\mathbf{u}\in\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{e}^{\ell}
.

For each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have a left action
\mathrel{\overset{k}{\bullet}}
of
\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{e}
on
{\mathbb{Z}}
defined by

\displaystyle\mathsf{s}_{i}\mathrel{\overset{k}{\bullet}}x

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}x-1,&\text{if }x\equiv_{e}i\\
x+1,&\text{if }x\equiv_{e}i-1\\
x,&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle(i\in[1,\,e-1]);

\displaystyle\mathbf{e}_{j}\mathrel{\overset{k}{\bullet}}x

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}x+ke,&\text{if }x\equiv_{e}j-1\\
x,&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle(j\in[1,\,e]).

We extend this left action
\mathrel{\overset{k}{\bullet}}
to the set
\mathcal{B}
of
\beta
-sets by
w\mathrel{\overset{k}{\bullet}}\mathsf{B}=\{w\mathrel{\overset{k}{\bullet}}b\mid b\in\mathsf{B}\}
for
w\in\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{e}
and
\mathsf{B}\in\mathcal{B}
.
For each
j\in\mathbb{Z}/e\mathbb{Z}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{s}(\mathsf{s}_{j}\mathrel{\overset{k}{\bullet}}\mathsf{B})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{s}(\mathsf{B});

\displaystyle\mathsf{wt}_{e}(\mathsf{s}_{j}\mathrel{\overset{k}{\bullet}}\mathsf{B})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{wt}_{e}(\mathsf{B});

\displaystyle\mathsf{core}_{e}(\mathsf{s}_{j}\mathrel{\overset{k}{\bullet}}\mathsf{B})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{s}_{j}\mathrel{\overset{k}{\bullet}}\mathsf{core}_{e}(\mathsf{B}).

We extend
\mathrel{\overset{k}{\bullet}}
further to
\mathcal{B}^{\ell}
by acting on each and every component.
Then

\mathbf{U}(\mathsf{s}_{j}\mathrel{\overset{1}{\bullet}}(\mathsf{B}^{(1)},\dotsc,\mathsf{B}^{(\ell)}))=\mathsf{s}_{j}\mathrel{\overset{\ell}{\bullet}}\mathbf{U}(\mathsf{B}^{(1)},\dotsc,\mathsf{B}^{(\ell)})

(2.6)

for all
j\in\mathbb{Z}/e\mathbb{Z}
by
[
LT24
, (2.16)]
.

For
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=(\lambda^{(1)},\dotsc,\lambda^{(\ell)})\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
and
\mathbf{t}=(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{\ell})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
, we have
a left action
\mathrel{\overset{\mathbf{t}}{\bullet}}
of
\mathbf{W}_{e}
on
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
,
where for each
j\in\mathbb{Z}/e\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathsf{s}_{j}\mathrel{\overset{\mathbf{t}}{\bullet}}\boldsymbol{\lambda}
is the
\ell
-partition obtained from
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
by removing all its removable nodes of
(e,\mathbf{t})
-residue
j
and adding all its addable nodes of
(e,\mathbf{t})
-residue
j
.
This satisfies

\beta_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathsf{s}_{j}\mathrel{\overset{\mathbf{t}}{\bullet}}\boldsymbol{\lambda})=\mathsf{s}_{j}\mathrel{\overset{1}{\bullet}}\beta_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})

(2.7)

by
[
LT24
, (2.17)]
,
and further induces a left action
\mathrel{\overset{}{\bullet}}
of
\mathbf{W}_{e}
on
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}\times{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
via
\mathsf{s}_{j}\mathrel{\overset{}{\bullet}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})=(\mathsf{s}_{j}\mathrel{\overset{\mathbf{t}}{\bullet}}\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})
for all
j\in\mathbb{Z}/e\mathbb{Z}
,
which commutes with the right action of
\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{\ell}
described above (interested readers please see last paragraph before Section 3 of
[
LT24
]
for details).

We also have a natural left action
\mathrel{\overset{k}{\circ}}
of
\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{e}
on
\mathcal{B}^{e}
and
\mathbb{Z}^{e}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathsf{s}_{i}\mathrel{\overset{k}{\circ}}(\mathsf{B}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{B}_{e})

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{B}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{B}_{i-1},\mathsf{B}_{i+1},\mathsf{B}_{i},\mathsf{B}_{i+2},\dotsc,\mathsf{B}_{e}),

\displaystyle\mathbf{e}_{j}\mathrel{\overset{k}{\circ}}(\mathsf{B}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{B}_{e})

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{B}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{B}_{j-1},\mathsf{B}_{j}^{+k},\mathsf{B}_{j+1},\dotsc,\mathsf{B}_{e}),

\displaystyle\mathsf{s}_{i}\mathrel{\overset{k}{\circ}}(a_{1},\dotsc,a_{e})

\displaystyle=(a_{1},\dotsc,a_{i-1},a_{i+1},a_{i},a_{i+2},\dotsc,a_{e}),

\displaystyle\mathbf{e}_{j}\mathrel{\overset{k}{\circ}}(a_{1},\dotsc,a_{e})

\displaystyle=(a_{1},\dotsc,a_{j-1},a_{j}+k,a_{j+1},\dotsc,a_{e}),

for all
i\in[1,\,e-1]
,
j\in[1,\,e]
,
(\mathsf{B}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{B}_{e})\in\mathcal{B}^{e}
and
(a_{1},\dotsc,a_{e})\in\mathbb{Z}^{e}
.
Note that
\mathrel{\overset{k}{\circ}}
and
\mathrel{\overset{k}{\bullet}}
are related in the following way:

\displaystyle w\mathrel{\overset{k}{\circ}}\mathsf{quot}_{e}(\mathsf{B})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{quot}_{e}(w\mathrel{\overset{k}{\bullet}}\mathsf{B}),

(2.8)

\displaystyle w\mathrel{\overset{k}{\circ}}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{s}}(\mathsf{quot}_{e}(\mathsf{B}))

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{s}}(w\mathrel{\overset{k}{\circ}}\mathsf{quot}_{e}(\mathsf{B}))=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{s}}(\mathsf{quot}_{e}(w\mathrel{\overset{k}{\bullet}}\mathsf{B}))

(2.9)

for all
w\in\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{e}
and
\mathsf{B}\in\mathcal{B}
(recall the function
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{s}}
in Definition
2.2
).

Note that when
k=0
, we have

\mathbf{t}\sigma\mathrel{\overset{0}{\circ}}(a_{1},\dotsc,a_{e})=(a_{\sigma^{-1}(1)},\dotsc,a_{\sigma^{-1}(e)})

for all
\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{Z}^{e}
and
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{e}
, so that
\mathrel{\overset{0}{\circ}}
is really just the left conjugation action of
\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{e}
on its Abelian normal subgroup
\mathbb{Z}^{e}
.

Lemma 2.8
.

(1)

Let
(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{u})\in\mathcal{P}^{e}\times\mathbb{Z}^{e}
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Then

w\mathrel{\overset{k}{\circ}}\beta_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\mu})=\beta_{\smash[t]{w\mathrel{\overset{k}{\circ}}\mathbf{u}}}(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\overline{w}^{-1}})

for all
w\in\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{e}
.
(Recall that
\overline{w}
denotes the projection of
w
onto
\mathfrak{S}_{e}
.)

(2)

Let
(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}\times\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
.
Then
(w\mathrel{\overset{\mathbf{t}}{\bullet}}\boldsymbol{\lambda})^{w^{\prime}}=w\mathrel{\overset{\mathbf{t}^{w^{\prime}}}{\bullet}}\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{w^{\prime}}
for all
w\in\mathbf{W}_{e}
and
w^{\prime}\in\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{\ell}
.

Proof.

It is straightforward to verify the statement for
w=\mathbf{e}_{i}
(
i\in[1,e]
) and
w=\mathsf{s}_{j}
(
j\in[1,e-1]
), and the general statement follows immediately.

For part (2), we have

(w\mathrel{\overset{\mathbf{t}^{w^{\prime}}}{\bullet}}\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{w^{\prime}};\mathbf{t}^{w^{\prime}})=w\mathrel{\overset{}{\bullet}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{w^{\prime}};\mathbf{t}^{w^{\prime}})=w\mathrel{\overset{}{\bullet}}((\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})^{w^{\prime}})=(w\mathrel{\overset{}{\bullet}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t}))^{w^{\prime}}=(w\mathrel{\overset{\mathbf{t}}{\bullet}}\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})^{w^{\prime}}=((w\mathrel{\overset{\mathbf{t}}{\bullet}}\boldsymbol{\lambda})^{w^{\prime}};\mathbf{t}^{w^{\prime}}),

so that
w\mathrel{\overset{\mathbf{t}^{w^{\prime}}}{\bullet}}\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{w^{\prime}}=(w\mathrel{\overset{\mathbf{t}}{\bullet}}\boldsymbol{\lambda})^{w^{\prime}}
as desired.
∎

2.7.
Ariki-Koike algebras

From now on,
\mathbb{F}
denotes a fixed field of characteristic
p
, where we allow
p=0
, and we assume that
\mathbb{F}
contains a primitive
e
-th root of unity if
e\neq p
.
Let
q\in\mathbb{F}
be such that
q=1
if
e=p
, and
q
is a primitive
e
-th root of unity otherwise.
Fix
\mathbf{r}=(r_{1},\dotsc,r_{\ell})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
, and let
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}
.
The
Ariki-Koike algebra

\mathcal{H}_{n}=\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{F},q,\mathbf{r}}(n)
is the unital
\mathbb{F}
-algebra generated by
\{T_{0},\dotsc,T_{n-1}\}
subject to the following relations:

\displaystyle(T_{0}-q^{r_{1}})(T_{0}-q^{r_{2}})\dotsm(T_{0}-q^{r_{\ell}})

\displaystyle=0;

\displaystyle(T_{a}-q)(T_{a}+1)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle(a\in[1,\,n-1]);

\displaystyle T_{0}T_{1}T_{0}T_{1}

\displaystyle=T_{1}T_{0}T_{1}T_{0};

\displaystyle T_{a}T_{a+1}T_{a}

\displaystyle=T_{a+1}T_{a}T_{a+1}

\displaystyle(a\in[1,\,n-2]);

\displaystyle T_{a}T_{b}

\displaystyle=T_{a}T_{b}

\displaystyle(|a-b|\geq 2).

It is clear from the definition that
\mathcal{H}_{n}
only depends on the orbit
\mathbf{r}^{\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{\ell}}
and not on
\mathbf{r}
.

The set of
Specht modules

\{S^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}=S^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{\mathbf{r}}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}(n)\}
is a distinguished class of
\mathcal{H}_{n}
-modules. Here, for our purposes, we use the Specht modules defined by
[
DR01
]
instead of the original ones constructed by Dipper, James and Mathas
[
DJM98
]
.
We note that, unlike
\mathcal{H}_{n}
, these Specht modules actually depend on the order of the
r_{j}
’s.

For
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
, define its

\mathcal{H}
-core
,
\mathcal{H}
-weight
and
\mathcal{H}
-hub
, denoted
\mathsf{core}_{\mathcal{H}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
,
\mathsf{wt}_{\mathcal{H}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
and
\mathsf{Hub}_{\mathcal{H}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})=(\mathsf{hub}^{\mathcal{H}}_{0}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}),\dotsc,\mathsf{hub}^{\mathcal{H}}_{e-1}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))
,
by

\displaystyle\mathsf{core}_{\mathcal{H}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})

\displaystyle=\beta^{-1}(\mathsf{core}_{e}((\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{r})^{w})),

\displaystyle\mathsf{wt}_{\mathcal{H}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{wt}_{e}((\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{r})^{w}),

\displaystyle\mathsf{Hub}_{\mathcal{H}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{Hub}_{e}((\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{r})^{w}),

where
w
is any element of
\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{\ell}
such that
\mathbf{r}^{w}\in\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{e}^{\ell}
.
By Theorem
2.7
,
\mathsf{core}_{\mathcal{H}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
and
\mathsf{wt}_{\mathcal{H}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
are well-defined, i.e. do not depend on the choice of
w
such that
\mathbf{r}^{w}\in\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{e}^{\ell}
, while
\mathsf{Hub}_{\mathcal{H}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
is well-defined by the definition of
\mathsf{Hub}_{e}
.

Given a block
B
of
\mathcal{H}_{n}
, we say that an
\ell
-partition
\boldsymbol{\lambda}

lies in
B
if
S^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
lies in
B
.

Theorem 2.9

(
[
LT24
, Corollary 4.5]
)
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
. Then
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
and
\boldsymbol{\mu}
lie in the same block of
\mathcal{H}_{n}
if and only if
\mathsf{core}_{\mathcal{H}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})=\mathsf{core}_{\mathcal{H}}(\boldsymbol{\mu})
and
\mathsf{wt}_{\mathcal{H}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})=\mathsf{wt}_{\mathcal{H}}(\boldsymbol{\mu})
.

By Theorem
2.9
,
we may unambiguously define the core and weight of a block
B
, denoted
\mathsf{core}(B)
and
\mathsf{wt}(B)
, as the common
\mathcal{H}
-core and the common
\mathcal{H}
-weight of the
\ell
-partitions lying in
B
. Moreover, these two attributes completely determine the block.

By (
2.3
),
\mathcal{H}
-hub is another block invariant of the Ariki-Koike algebras, and we write
\mathsf{Hub}(B)=(\mathsf{hub}_{0}(B),\dotsc,\mathsf{hub}_{e-1}(B))
for the common
\mathcal{H}
-hub of the
\ell
-partitions lying in
B
.

Recall that we have a left action
\mathrel{\overset{\mathbf{r}}{\bullet}}
of
\mathbf{W}_{e}
on
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
.
This induces a left action
\mathrel{\overset{}{\bullet}}
of
\mathbf{W}_{e}
on the set of blocks of Ariki-Koike algebras (with common
\ell
-charge
\mathbf{r}
) such that if
B
is a block of
\mathcal{H}_{n}
, then
\mathsf{s}_{j}\mathrel{\overset{}{\bullet}}B
is the block of
\mathcal{H}_{n-\mathsf{hub}_{j}(B)}
with

\mathsf{core}(\mathsf{s}_{j}\mathrel{\overset{}{\bullet}}B)=\beta^{-1}(\mathsf{s}_{j}\mathrel{\overset{\ell}{\bullet}}\beta_{|\mathbf{r}|}(\mathsf{core}(B)))\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{s}_{j}\mathrel{\overset{}{\bullet}}B)=\mathsf{wt}(B)

(see, for example,
[
LT24
, Proposition 4.8]
).

3.
Moving Vectors

The concept of moving vectors was first introduced by the first and second authors in
[
LQ23
]
for
\ell
-partitions with associated
\ell
-charge lying in
\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{e}^{\ell}
to classify the blocks of Ariki-Koike algebras with finite representation type.

In this section, we will use moving vectors to classify the core blocks of Ariki-Koike algebras, and obtain some immediate consequences for the weight graphs for
\ell
-partitions.

3.1.
Definition and properties

We present in this subsection a slightly different perspective of moving vectors, generalising it to all
\ell
-charges.

Recall the Uglov map
\mathbf{U}
in Subsection
2.5
. Post-composing
\mathbf{U}
with
\mathsf{quot}_{e}
gives a bijection from
\mathcal{B}^{\ell}
to
\mathcal{B}^{e}
. Together with the bijection
\beta
between
\mathcal{P}\times\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathcal{B}
, this further induces a bijection
\iota_{\ell,e}:\mathcal{P}^{\ell}\times\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}\to\mathcal{P}^{e}\times\mathbb{Z}^{e}
defined by
\iota_{\ell,e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})=(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{u})
where
\mathsf{quot}_{e}(\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})))=\beta_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\mu})
. We like to think of
\iota_{\ell,e}
as the
rank-level duality map
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}\times\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
, and let
\iota_{\ell,e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})=(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{u})
(thus
(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{u})\in\mathcal{P}^{e}\times\mathbb{Z}^{e}
with
\beta_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\mu})=\mathsf{quot}_{e}(\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})))
). Then

(1)

\mathsf{quot}_{e}(\mathsf{core}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t}))=\beta_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\varnothing})
;

(2)

\mathsf{res}^{\mathbf{u}}_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\mu})
is completely determined by
\mathbf{t}
,
\mathsf{core}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})
and
\mathsf{wt}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})
;

(3)

\iota_{\ell,e}((\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})^{\rho_{\ell}\mathbf{e}_{\ell}})=(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{u}+\boldsymbol{1})
, where
\rho_{\ell}=(1,\dotsc,\ell)\in\mathfrak{S}_{\ell}
.

Proof.

Part (1) is clear.

For part (2), let
(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*};\mathbf{t}^{*})\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}\times\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
be such that
\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*}))=\mathsf{core}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})
.
Note that a removable node of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
with
(\ell,\mathbf{u})
-residue
i
corresponds to an
x\in\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))
such that
\lfloor x/e\rfloor\equiv_{\ell}i
and
x-e\notin\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))
,
i.e. an
x\in\upsilon_{\ell-i}(\beta_{t_{\ell-i}}(\lambda^{(\ell-i)}))
such that
x-e\notin\upsilon_{\ell-i+1}(\beta_{t_{\ell-i+1}}(\lambda^{(\ell-i+1)}))
(where
\ell-i+1
is to be read as
1
if
i=0
).
Removing this node from
\boldsymbol{\mu}
thus corresponds to removing
\upsilon_{\ell-i}^{-1}(x)
from
\beta_{t_{\ell-i}}(\lambda^{(\ell-i)})
and adding
\upsilon_{\ell-i+1}^{-1}(x-e)
to
\beta_{t_{\ell-i+1}}(\lambda^{(\ell-i+1)})
.
Consequently, this results in subtracting 1 from the charge of
\beta_{t_{\ell-i}}(\lambda^{(\ell-i)})
and adding 1 to the charge of
\beta_{t_{\ell-i+1}}(\lambda^{(\ell-i+1)})
.
Since repeatedly removing the removable nodes from
\boldsymbol{\mu}
eventually arrives at
\boldsymbol{\varnothing}
, and

\beta_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\varnothing})=\mathsf{quot}_{e}(\mathsf{core}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t}))=\mathsf{quot}_{e}(\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*}))),

we have
t^{*}_{\ell-i}=t_{\ell-i}-C_{i}+C_{i+1}
, where
C_{k}
is the total number of nodes of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
with
(\ell,\mathbf{u})
-residue
k
for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}/\ell\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathbf{t}^{*}=(t^{*}_{1},\dotsc,t^{*}_{\ell})
. This is true for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}/\ell\mathbb{Z}
, so that we get a system of
\ell
linear equations in
C_{i}
’s which has a one-dimensional solution space. However, we further require that

\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}C_{i}=\mathsf{wt}_{e}(\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})))=\mathsf{wt}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t}),

which is a linear equation in
C_{i}
’s independent of the earlier linear equations.
Thus the
C_{i}
’s, and hence
\mathsf{res}^{\mathbf{u}}_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\mu})
, have a unique solution when
\mathbf{t}
,
\mathsf{wt}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})
and
\mathsf{core}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})
are fixed.

For part (3), we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{quot}_{e}(\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}^{\rho_{\ell}\mathbf{e}_{\ell}}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\rho_{\ell}\mathbf{e}_{\ell}})))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{quot}_{e}((\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})))^{+e})

\displaystyle(\text{by Lemma \ref{L:AA}(4)})

\displaystyle=((\beta_{u_{1}}(\mu^{(1)}))^{+1},\dotsc,(\beta_{u_{e}}(\mu^{(e)}))^{+1})

\displaystyle(\text{by \eqref{E:quot}})

\displaystyle=(\beta_{u_{1}+1}(\mu^{(1)}),\dotsc,\beta_{u_{e}+1}(\mu^{(e)}))=\beta_{\mathbf{u}+\boldsymbol{1}}(\boldsymbol{\mu}).

Thus
\iota_{\ell,e}((\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})^{\rho_{\ell}\mathbf{e}_{\ell}})=(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{u}+\boldsymbol{1})
.
∎

Definition 3.2
.

Let
(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}\times\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
. The
e
-moving vector of
(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})
, denoted
\mathbf{mv}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})
, is defined as

\mathbf{mv}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t}):=(C_{\ell-1},C_{\ell-2},\dotsc,C_{0}),

where
C_{i}
is the total number of nodes of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
with
(\ell,\mathbf{u})
-residue
i
and
\iota_{\ell,e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})=(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{u})
.

Example 3.3
.

Let
e=5
,
\ell=4
,
\mathbf{t}=(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3},t_{4})=(1,3,3,6)
, and
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=(\lambda^{(1)},\lambda^{(2)},\lambda^{(3)},\lambda^{(4)})=((3,2,1^{4}),(4,2,1),(2^{2},1),(1))
.

\beta_{t_{1}}(\lambda^{(1)})
\beta_{t_{2}}(\lambda^{(2)})
\beta_{t_{3}}(\lambda^{(3)})
\beta_{t_{3}}(\lambda^{(4)})
\dotsm
\dotsm
\dotsm
\dotsm
\dotsm
\dotsm
\dotsm
\dotsm
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))

Thus
\iota_{4,5}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})=(((1),(1),\varnothing,\varnothing,\varnothing);(0,6,1,4,2))
, and
\mathbf{mv}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})=(0,1,0,1)
.

Remark 3.4
.

Let
(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}\times\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
with
\iota_{\ell,e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})=(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{u})
.
A node
(a,b,j)
of
[\boldsymbol{\mu}]
corresponds bijectively to a triple
(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}
with
x<y\leq z
and
x\equiv_{e}y\equiv_{e}z
such that
x\notin\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))
,
z\in\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))
, and

y=\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{u}}(a,b,j)e+j-1
.
Since
\mathsf{res}^{\mathbf{u}}_{\ell}(a,b,j)\equiv_{\ell}\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{u}}(a,b,j)
,
we see that
(a,b,j)
has
(\ell,\mathbf{u})
-residue
i
if and only if
y\in\upsilon_{\ell-i}(\mathbb{Z})
.
From this description of nodes of
[\boldsymbol{\mu}]
with
(\ell,\mathbf{u})
-residue
i
, one can see that our definition of the moving vector is equivalent to that first defined in
[
LQ23
]
, even though the latter only does so for
\mathbf{t}\in\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{e}^{\ell}
.

We record the following result which is already obtained in the proof of Lemma
3.1
(2).

Corollary 3.5
.

Let
(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}\times\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
, and let
\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*}))=\mathsf{core}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})
. Then

\mathbf{t}^{*}=(t^{*}_{1},\dotsc,t^{*}_{\ell})
satisfies

t^{*}_{i}=t_{i}-C_{\ell-i}+C_{\ell-i+1}

for all
i\in[1,\,\ell]
, where
C_{k}
is the number of nodes of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
with
(\ell,\mathbf{u})
-residue
k
and
\iota_{\ell,e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})=(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{u})
, and
\ell-i+1
is to be read as
0
if
i=1
.

In particular,
\mathbf{t}^{*}
is completely determined by
\mathbf{t}
and
\mathbf{mv}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})
.

The following lemma generalises
[
LQ23
, Lemma 4.3.1]
:

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=(\lambda^{(1)},\dotsc,\lambda^{(\ell)})\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
and let
\mathbf{t}=(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{\ell})\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
.
Let
k_{1},\dotsc,k_{\ell-1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and define

\mathbf{u}=(u_{1},\dotsc,u_{\ell})\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
by

u_{i}:=t_{i}+k_{i}-k_{i-1}

for all
i\in[1,\,\ell]
, where
k_{0}=k_{\ell}=0
.
If
t_{1}\leq\dotsb\leq t_{\ell}
and
u_{1}\leq\dotsb\leq u_{\ell}
, then there exists
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
such that
\mathsf{core}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{u})=\mathsf{core}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})
and
\mathbf{mv}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{u})=\mathbf{mv}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})+(k_{1},\dotsc,k_{\ell-1},0)
.

Proof.

Note first that
\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}u_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}t_{i}
.

We prove by induction on
K=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell-1}k_{i}
.
If
K=0
, then
k_{i}=0
for all
i\in[1,\,\ell-1]
, so that
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{t}
, and hence we can let
\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{\lambda}
.
If
K>0
, let
M=\mathrm{max}\{i\in[1,\,\ell-1]\mid k_{i}>0\}
.
Then
u_{M+1}=t_{M+1}-k_{M}<t_{M+1}
, and
u_{i}=t_{i}
for all
i>M+1
.
Since
\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}u_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}t_{i}
, there exists
j\in[1,\,\ell]
such that
u_{j}>t_{j}
, and we let
m
be the largest such index.
Then
m\leq M
,
so that

t_{m}<u_{m}\leq u_{M+1}<t_{M+1}
, hence

t_{m}+1\leq u_{m}\leq u_{M+1}\leq t_{M+1}-1.

Let

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{max}\{i\in[1,\ell]\mid t_{i}=t_{m}\};

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{min}\{i\in[1,\ell]\mid t_{i}=t_{M+1}\}.

Then
m\leq a<b\leq M+1
, and
t_{a}<t_{i}<t_{b}
for all
i\in[a+1,\,b-1]
.
Let
\beta_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})=(\mathsf{B}^{(1)},\dotsc,\mathsf{B}^{(\ell)})
.
Since
\mathcal{s}(\mathsf{B}^{(a)})=t_{a}=t_{m}<t_{M+1}=t_{b}=\mathcal{s}(\mathsf{B}^{(b)})
,
there exists
x\in\mathsf{B}^{(b)}
such that
x\notin\mathsf{B}^{(a)}
by Lemma
2.1
(1).
For
i\in[1,\,\ell]
, let

\mathsf{C}^{(i)}=\begin{cases}\mathsf{B}^{(a)}\cup\{x\},&\text{if }i=a;\\
\mathsf{B}^{(b)}\setminus\{x\},&\text{if }i=b;\\
\mathsf{B}^{(i)},&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Let
(\boldsymbol{\nu};\mathbf{v})\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}\times\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
be such that
\beta_{\mathbf{v}}(\boldsymbol{\nu})=(\mathsf{C}^{(1)},\dotsc,\mathsf{C}^{(\ell)})
.
Then

\displaystyle\mathsf{core}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\nu};\mathbf{v})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{core}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t}),\qquad\mathbf{mv}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\nu};\mathbf{v})=\mathbf{mv}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})+\smash{\sum_{i=a}^{b-1}}\mathbf{e}_{i}.

If
\mathbf{v}=(v_{1},\dotsc,v_{\ell})
, then
v_{i}=t_{i}+\delta_{ia}-\delta_{ib}
for all
i\in[1,\ell]
, and so

v_{i+1}-v_{i}=t_{i+1}-t_{i}+\delta_{i,a-1}-\delta_{ia}+\delta_{ib}-\delta_{i,b-1}\geq 0

for all
i\in[1,\,\ell-1]
,
since
t_{a+1}>t_{a}
and
t_{b}>t_{b-1}
and
t_{b}-t_{a}=t_{M}-t_{m}\geq 2
.
Moreover, if
i>a
, then
i>m
, so that
t_{i}\geq u_{i}
by definition of
m
.
Since
u_{i}=t_{i}+k_{i}-k_{i-1}
, this yields
k_{i-1}\geq k_{i}
when
i>a
.
In particular, we have

k_{a}\geq k_{a+1}\geq\dotsb\geq k_{M}>0.

For each
i\in[1,\,\ell-1]
,
let
k^{\prime}_{i}:=k_{i}-\mathbbm{1}_{i\in[a,\,b-1]}
.
Then
k^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and

\displaystyle u_{i}=t_{i}+k_{i}-k_{i-1}=(v_{i}-\delta_{ia}+\delta_{ib})+(k^{\prime}_{i}-k^{\prime}_{i-1}+\delta_{ia}-\delta_{ib})=v_{i}+k^{\prime}_{i}-k^{\prime}_{i-1}

for all
i\in[1,\,\ell]
, where
k^{\prime}_{0}=k^{\prime}_{\ell}=0
.
Since
\sum_{i=1}^{\ell-1}k^{\prime}_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell-1}k_{i}-(b-a)<\sum_{i=1}^{\ell-1}k_{i}
, by induction, there exists
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
such that

\displaystyle\mathsf{core}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{u})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{core}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\nu};\mathbf{v})=\mathsf{core}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t});

\displaystyle\mathbf{mv}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{u})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{mv}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\nu};\mathbf{v})+(k^{\prime}_{1},\dotsc,k^{\prime}_{\ell-1},0)=\mathbf{mv}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})+(k_{1},\dotsc,k_{\ell-1},0),

as desired.
∎

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}\times\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
, and let
\rho_{\ell}=(1,2,\dotsc,\ell)\in\mathfrak{S}_{\ell}
.

(1)

Then

\mathbf{mv}_{e}((\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})^{\rho_{\ell}\mathbf{e}_{\ell}})=(\mathbf{mv}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t}))^{\rho_{\ell}}.

(2)

If
w,w^{\prime}\in\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{\ell}
such that
\mathbf{t}^{w},\mathbf{t}^{w^{\prime}}\in\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{e}^{\ell}
, then

\mathbf{mv}_{e}((\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})^{w^{\prime}})=(\mathbf{mv}_{e}((\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})^{w}))^{\rho_{\ell}^{k}}

for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}/\ell\mathbb{Z}
.

(3)

If
w,w^{\prime}\in\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{\ell}
such that
\mathbf{t}^{w}=\mathbf{t}^{w^{\prime}}\in\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{e}^{\ell}
, then

\mathbf{mv}_{e}((\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})^{w})=\mathbf{mv}_{e}((\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})^{w^{\prime}}).

Proof.

(1)

Let
\iota_{\ell,e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})=(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{u})
.
By Lemma
3.1
(3),
\iota_{\ell,e}((\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})^{\rho_{\ell}\mathbf{e}_{\ell}})=(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{u}+\boldsymbol{1})
.
A node of
[\boldsymbol{\mu}]
has
(\ell;\mathbf{u})
-residue
i
if and only if it has
(\ell;\mathbf{u}+\boldsymbol{1})
-residue
i+1\pmod{\ell}
.
Consequently, if
C_{i}(\boldsymbol{\nu};\mathbf{v})
denotes the number of nodes of
[\boldsymbol{\nu}]
of
(\ell,\mathbf{v})
-residue
i
, then
C_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{u})=C_{i+1}(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{u}+\boldsymbol{1})
.
Thus,

\displaystyle\mathbf{mv}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})

\displaystyle=(C_{\ell-1}(\iota_{\ell,e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})),C_{\ell-2}(\iota_{\ell,e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})),\dotsc,C_{0}(\iota_{\ell,e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})))

\displaystyle=(C_{\ell-1}(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{u}),C_{\ell-2}(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{u}),\dotsc,C_{0}(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{u}))

\displaystyle=(C_{0}(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{u}+\boldsymbol{1}),C_{\ell-1}(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{u}+\boldsymbol{1}),\dotsc,C_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{u}+\boldsymbol{1}))

\displaystyle=(C_{\ell-1}(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{u}+\boldsymbol{1}),C_{\ell-2}(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{u}+\boldsymbol{1}),\dotsc,C_{0}(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{u}+\boldsymbol{1}))^{\rho_{\ell}^{-1}}

\displaystyle=(C_{\ell-1}(\iota_{\ell,e}((\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})^{\rho_{\ell}\mathbf{e}_{\ell}})),C_{\ell-2}(\iota_{\ell,e}((\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})^{\rho_{\ell}\mathbf{e}_{\ell}})),\dotsc,C_{0}(\iota_{\ell,e}((\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})^{\rho_{\ell}\mathbf{e}_{\ell}})))^{\rho_{\ell}^{-1}}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{mv}_{e}((\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})^{\rho_{\ell}\mathbf{e}_{\ell}}))^{\rho_{\ell}^{-1}}

as desired.

(2)

By Lemma
2.6
(5),
\mathbf{t}^{w^{\prime}}=(\mathbf{t}^{w})^{(\rho_{\ell}\mathbf{e}_{\ell})^{k}}
for some unique
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Consequently,

\mathbf{mv}_{e}((\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})^{w^{\prime}})=\mathbf{mv}_{e}(((\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})^{w})^{(\rho_{\ell}\mathbf{e}_{\ell})^{k}})=(\mathbf{mv}_{e}((\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})^{w}))^{\rho_{\ell}^{k}}

by part (1). Since
\rho_{\ell}
has order
\ell
,
\mathbf{mv}_{e}((\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})^{w^{\prime}})=(\mathbf{mv}_{e}((\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})^{w}))^{\rho_{\ell}^{k\textrm{ mod }\ell}}
as desired.

(3)

By Theorem
2.7
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{core}_{e}((\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})^{w})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{core}_{e}((\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})^{w^{\prime}}),

\displaystyle\mathsf{wt}_{e}((\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})^{w})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{min}\{\mathsf{wt}_{e}((\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})^{\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{\ell}})\}=\mathsf{wt}_{e}((\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})^{w^{\prime}}).

By Lemma
3.1
,
\mathbf{mv}_{e}((\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})^{w})
and
\mathbf{mv}_{e}((\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})^{w^{\prime}})
are both completely determined by
\mathbf{t}^{w}=\mathbf{t}^{w^{\prime}}
,
\mathsf{core}_{e}((\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})^{w})=\mathsf{core}_{e}((\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})^{w^{\prime}})
and
\mathsf{wt}_{e}((\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})^{w})=\mathsf{wt}_{e}((\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})^{w^{\prime}})
.
Thus,

\mathbf{mv}_{e}((\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})^{w})=\mathbf{mv}_{e}((\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})^{w^{\prime}}).

∎

Lemmas
3.6
and
3.7
(1) give us the following partial converse to Corollary
3.5
, which is a generalisation of
[
LQ23
, Lemma 4.3.2 and Remark 4.3.3]
.

Corollary 3.8
.

Let
(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*};\mathbf{t}^{*})\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}\times\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
be such that
\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*}))
is an
e
-core.
Let
\mathbf{m}=(m_{1},\dotsc,m_{\ell})\in(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})^{\ell}
, and suppose that
\mathbf{t}=(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{\ell})\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
satisfies

t_{i}=t^{*}_{i}+m_{i}-m_{i-1}
for all
i\in[1,\,\ell]
, where
\mathbf{t}^{*}=(t^{*}_{1},\dotsc,t^{*}_{\ell})
and
m_{0}=m_{\ell}
.
If
t_{1}\leq\dotsb\leq t_{\ell}
, then there exists
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=(\lambda^{(1)},\dotsc,\lambda^{(\ell)})\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
such that

\mathsf{core}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})=\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*}))
and

\mathbf{mv}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})=\mathbf{m}
. Furthermore,
\mathsf{wt}_{e}(\lambda^{(i)})\geq\mathrm{min}\{m_{1},\dotsc,m_{\ell}\}
for some
i\in[1,\,\ell]
.

Proof.

Since
\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*}))
is an
e
-core,
\mathbf{t}^{*}\in\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{e}^{\ell}
by
[
JL21
, Proposition 2.14]
.
In particular,
t^{*}_{1}\leq\dotsb\leq t^{*}_{\ell}
.
We note also that
|\mathbf{t}|=|\mathbf{t}^{*}|
.

We assume first that
m_{\ell}\leq m_{i}
for all
i\in[1,\ell]
.
Let
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}=(m^{\prime}_{1},\dotsc,m^{\prime}_{\ell}):=\mathbf{m}-m_{\ell}\boldsymbol{1}
.
Then
m^{\prime}_{\ell}=0
, and
m^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and

t_{i}=t^{*}_{i}+m^{\prime}_{i}-m^{\prime}_{i-1}
for all
i\in[1,\,\ell]
.
Thus, there exists
\boldsymbol{\mu}=(\mu^{(1)},\dotsc,\mu^{(\ell)})\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
such that

\displaystyle\mathsf{core}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{t})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{core}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*};\mathbf{t}^{*})=\mathsf{core}_{e}(\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*})))=\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*})),

\displaystyle\mathbf{mv}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{t})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{mv}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*};\mathbf{t}^{*})+\mathbf{m}^{\prime}=\mathbf{m}^{\prime}

by Lemma
3.6
.
Let
\nu
be the partition obtained from
\mu^{(\ell)}
by adding
m_{\ell}e
nodes to its first row.
Then
\mathsf{wt}_{e}(\nu)\geq m_{\ell}
.
Let
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=(\mu^{(1)},\dotsc,\mu^{(\ell-1)},\nu)
.
Then
\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))
can be obtained from
\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\mu}))
by replacing
\upsilon_{\ell}(x)
with
\upsilon_{\ell}(x+m_{\ell}e)=\upsilon_{\ell}(x)+m_{\ell}e\ell
, where
x=\mathrm{max}(\beta_{t_{\ell}}(\mu^{(\ell)}))
.
Consequently,

\displaystyle\mathsf{core}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{core}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{t})=\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*}));

\displaystyle\mathbf{mv}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{mv}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{t})+m_{\ell}\boldsymbol{1}=\mathbf{m^{\prime}}+m_{\ell}\boldsymbol{1}=\mathbf{m}.

In general, there exists
i\in[1,\ell]
such that
m_{i}\leq m_{j}
for all
j\in[1,\,\ell]
.
Then
(\mathbf{t}^{*})^{(\rho_{\ell}\mathbf{e}_{\ell})^{i}}\in\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{e}^{\ell}
by Lemma
2.6
(2).
Thus

\mathsf{wt}_{e}((\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*};\mathbf{t}^{*})^{(\rho_{\ell}\mathbf{e}_{\ell})^{i}})=\mathrm{min}(\mathsf{wt}_{e}((\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*};\mathbf{t}^{*})^{\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{\ell}}))=\mathsf{wt}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*};\mathbf{t}^{*})=0

by Theorem
2.7
(2),
so that

\mathbf{U}(\beta_{(\mathbf{t}^{*})^{(\rho_{\ell}\mathbf{e}_{\ell})^{i}}}((\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*})^{\rho_{\ell}^{i}}))
is an
e
-core.
Let

\displaystyle\mathbf{m}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(m^{\prime}_{1},\dotsc,m^{\prime}_{\ell})

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{m}^{\rho_{\ell}^{i}};

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}

\displaystyle=(u_{1},\dotsc,u_{\ell})

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{t}^{(\rho_{\ell}\mathbf{e}_{\ell})^{i}};

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}^{*}

\displaystyle=(u^{*}_{1},\dotsc,u^{*}_{\ell})

\displaystyle:=(\mathbf{t}^{*})^{(\rho_{\ell}\mathbf{e}_{\ell})^{i}}.

Then
m^{\prime}_{j}=m_{j+i\textrm{ mod }\ell}
,

u_{j}=t_{j+i\textrm{ mod }\ell}+e\mathbbm{1}_{j+i>\ell}

and
u^{*}_{j}=t^{*}_{j+i\textrm{ mod }\ell}+e\mathbbm{1}_{j+i>\ell}
for all
j\in[1,\,\ell]
.
In particular,
m^{\prime}_{\ell}=m_{i}\leq m^{\prime}_{j}
and

\displaystyle u_{j}

\displaystyle=t_{j+i\textrm{ mod }\ell}+e\mathbbm{1}_{j+i>\ell}

\displaystyle=t^{*}_{j+i\textrm{ mod }\ell}+m_{j+i\textrm{ mod }\ell}-m_{j+i-1\textrm{ mod }\ell}+e\mathbbm{1}_{j+i>\ell}

\displaystyle=u^{*}_{j}+m^{\prime}_{j}-m^{\prime}_{j-1}

for all
j\in[1,\,\ell]
.
Consequently, we can conclude from above that there exists
\boldsymbol{\mu}=(\mu^{(1)},\dotsc,\mu^{(\ell)})\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
such that
\mathsf{core}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{u})=\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{u}^{*}}((\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*})^{\rho_{\ell}^{i}}))
,

\mathbf{mv}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{u})=\mathbf{m}^{\prime}
and
\mathsf{wt}_{e}(\mu^{(\ell)})\geq m^{\prime}_{\ell}=m_{i}
.
Let
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\rho_{\ell}^{-i}}
.
Then

\mathbf{mv}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})=\mathbf{mv}_{e}((\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{u})^{(\rho_{\ell}\mathbf{e}_{\ell})^{-i}})=(\mathbf{mv}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{u}))^{\rho_{\ell}^{-i}}=(\mathbf{m}^{\prime})^{\rho_{\ell}^{-i}}=\mathbf{m},

where the second equality follows from Lemma
3.7
(1).
Furthermore,

\displaystyle\beta^{-1}(\mathsf{core}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t}))

\displaystyle=\beta^{-1}(\mathsf{core}_{e}((\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{u})^{(\rho_{\ell}\mathbf{e}_{\ell})^{-i}}))=\beta^{-1}(\mathsf{core}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{u}))=\beta^{-1}(\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{u}^{*}}((\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*})^{\rho_{\ell}^{i}})))

\displaystyle=\beta^{-1}(\mathsf{core}_{e}((\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*};\mathbf{t}^{*})^{(\rho_{\ell}\mathbf{e}_{\ell})^{i}}))=\beta^{-1}(\mathsf{core}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*};\mathbf{r}^{*}))=\beta^{-1}(\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*})))

by Theorem
2.7
(1),
so that
\mathsf{core}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})=\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*}))
since
\mathcal{s}(\mathsf{core}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t}))=|\mathbf{t}|=|\mathbf{t}^{*}|=\mathcal{s}(\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*})))
.
In addition,
\mathsf{wt}_{e}(\lambda^{(i)})=\mathsf{wt}_{e}(\mu^{(\rho_{\ell}^{-i}(i))})=\mathsf{wt}_{e}(\mu^{(\ell)})\geq m_{i}
, where
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=(\lambda^{(1)},\dotsc,\lambda^{(\ell)})
.
∎

Recall the left action
\mathrel{\overset{}{\bullet}}
of
\mathbf{W}_{e}
on
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}\times\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
as described in Subsection
2.6
.

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}\times\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
.

(1)

There exists
(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{t})\in\mathbf{W}_{e}\mathrel{\overset{}{\bullet}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})
such that
\mathsf{hub}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{t})\leq 0
for all
j\in\mathbb{Z}/e\mathbb{Z}
.

(2)

For each
j\in\mathbb{Z}/e\mathbb{Z}
,

\mathbf{mv}_{e}(\mathsf{s}_{j}\mathrel{\overset{}{\bullet}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t}))=\mathbf{mv}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})
.

Proof.

(1)

Note first that
\mathbf{W}_{e}\mathrel{\overset{}{\bullet}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})=(\mathbf{W}_{e}\mathrel{\overset{\mathbf{t}}{\bullet}}\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})
. Let
\boldsymbol{\mu}
be an
\ell
-partition in the orbit
\mathbf{W}_{e}\mathrel{\overset{\mathbf{t}}{\bullet}}\boldsymbol{\lambda}
with the least size. If
\mathsf{hub}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{t})>0
, then
\mathsf{s}_{j}\mathrel{\overset{\mathbf{t}}{\bullet}}\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathbf{W}_{e}\mathrel{\overset{\mathbf{t}}{\bullet}}\boldsymbol{\lambda}
, and
\mathsf{s}_{j}\mathrel{\overset{\mathbf{t}}{\bullet}}\boldsymbol{\mu}
has size
|\boldsymbol{\mu}|-\mathsf{hub}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{t})<|\boldsymbol{\mu}|
, contradicting our choice of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
.

(2)

Let
\iota_{\ell,e}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})=(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{u})
and
\iota_{\ell,e}(\mathsf{s}_{j}\mathrel{\overset{}{\bullet}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t}))=(\boldsymbol{\nu};\mathbf{v})
. Then

\displaystyle\mathsf{quot}_{e}(\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})))

\displaystyle=\beta_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\mu})

\displaystyle=(\beta_{u_{1}}(\mu^{(1)}),\dotsc,\beta_{u_{e}}(\mu^{(e)})),

\displaystyle\mathsf{quot}_{e}(\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathsf{s}_{i}\mathrel{\overset{\mathbf{t}}{\bullet}}\boldsymbol{\lambda})))

\displaystyle=\beta_{\mathbf{v}}(\boldsymbol{\nu})

\displaystyle=(\beta_{v_{1}}(\nu^{(1)}),\dotsc,\beta_{v_{e}}(\nu^{(e)})).

Since
\mathsf{s}_{j}\mathrel{\overset{}{\bullet}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})=(\mathsf{s}_{j}\mathrel{\overset{\mathbf{t}}{\bullet}}\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})
, and

\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathsf{s}_{j}\mathrel{\overset{\mathbf{t}}{\bullet}}\boldsymbol{\lambda}))=\mathbf{U}(\mathsf{s}_{j}\mathrel{\overset{1}{\bullet}}\beta_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))=\mathsf{s}_{j}\mathrel{\overset{\ell}{\bullet}}\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))
by (
2.7
) and (
2.6
) respectively,
we have

\beta_{\mathbf{v}}(\boldsymbol{\nu})=\mathsf{quot}_{e}(\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathsf{s}_{j}\mathrel{\overset{\mathbf{t}}{\bullet}}\boldsymbol{\lambda})))=\mathsf{quot}_{e}(\mathsf{s}_{j}\mathrel{\overset{\ell}{\bullet}}\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})))=\mathsf{s}_{j}\mathrel{\overset{\ell}{\circ}}\mathsf{quot}_{e}(\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{t}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})))=\mathsf{s}_{j}\mathrel{\overset{\ell}{\circ}}\beta_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\mu}),

by (
2.8
), so that

\beta_{v_{j^{\prime}}}(\nu^{(j^{\prime})})=\beta_{u_{j^{\prime}}}(\mu^{(j^{\prime})})
for all
j^{\prime}\in[1,\,e]\setminus\{j,j+1\}
, while

\displaystyle\beta_{v_{j}}(\nu^{(j)})

\displaystyle=(\beta_{u_{j+1}}(\mu^{(j+1)}))^{+(-\delta_{j0}\ell)}

\displaystyle=\beta_{u_{j+1}-\delta_{je}\ell}(\mu^{(j+1)}),

\displaystyle\beta_{v_{j+1}}(\nu^{(j+1)})

\displaystyle=(\beta_{u_{j}}(\mu^{(j)}))^{+\delta_{j0}\ell}

\displaystyle=\beta_{u_{j}+\delta_{je}\ell}(\mu^{(j)}),

where
u_{j}
,
v_{j}
and
\mu^{(j)}
and
\nu^{(j)}
are to be read as
u_{e}
,
v_{e}
and
\mu^{(e)}
and
\nu^{(e)}
respectively when
j=0
.
Consequently,
