t_{a_{1}}<t_{i_{1}}
by (
4.1
) and (
4.2
), so there exists
y\in\beta_{t_{i_{1}}}(\mu^{(i_{1})})\setminus\beta_{t_{a_{1}}}(\mu^{(a_{1})})
.
If
a_{2}>i_{2}+1
, then
t_{i_{2}+1}<t_{a_{2}}
by (
4.1
) and (
4.3
), so there exists
y^{\prime}\in\beta_{t_{a_{2}}}(\mu^{(a_{2})})\setminus\beta_{t_{i_{2}+1}}(\mu^{(i_{2}+1)})
.
Note that
\{y,y^{\prime}\}\cap\{x,x+1\}=\emptyset
since
x,x+1\in\beta_{t_{a_{1}}}(\mu^{(a_{1})})\setminus\beta_{t_{a_{2}}}(\mu^{(a_{2})})
.
Define
\boldsymbol{\nu}=(\nu^{(1)},\dotsc,\nu^{(\ell)})\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
by

\beta_{r^{\prime}_{c}}(\nu^{(c)})=\begin{cases}\beta_{t_{i_{1}}}(\mu^{(i_{1})})\setminus\{x+1\},&\text{if }c=i_{1}=a_{1};\\
\beta_{t_{i_{1}}}(\mu^{(i_{1})})\setminus\{y\},&\text{if }c=i_{1}>a_{1};\\
\beta_{t_{a_{1}}}(\mu^{(a_{1})})\cup\{y\}\setminus\{x+1\},&\text{if }c=a_{1}<i_{1};\\
\beta_{t_{a_{2}}}(\mu^{(a_{2})})\cup\{x+1\}\setminus\{y^{\prime}\},&\text{if }c=a_{2}>i_{2}+1;\\
\beta_{t_{i_{2}+1}}(\mu^{(i_{2}+1)})\cup\{x+1\},&\text{if }c=i_{2}+1=a_{2};\\
\beta_{t_{i_{2}+1}}(\mu^{(i_{2}+1)})\cup\{y^{\prime}\},&\text{if }c=i_{2}+1<a_{2};\\
\beta_{t_{c}}(\mu^{(c)}),&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Then
\nu^{(a_{1})}
has an addable node with
(e,r^{\prime}_{a_{1}})
-residue
\equiv_{e}j
while
\nu^{(a_{2})}
has a removable node with
(e,r^{\prime}_{a_{2}})
-residue
\equiv_{e}j
, with both nodes corresponding to
x+1
.
Thus
\boldsymbol{\nu}
has an addable node and a removable node, both with
(e,\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}})
-residue
\equiv_{e}j
.
Similar to how we showed that
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{k}^{{w_{0}}^{-1}}
lies in
B
, it is straightforward to verify that
\boldsymbol{\nu}^{{w_{0}}^{-1}}
also lies in
B
. Let
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=\boldsymbol{\nu}^{{w_{0}}^{-1}}
.
Then
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
lies in
B
and has an addable node and a removable node, both with
(e,\mathbf{r})
-residue
\equiv_{e}j
.

Case 3.
\boldsymbol{\mu}
has no removable node with
(e,\mathbf{t})
-residue
\equiv_{e}j
:

This is similar to Case 2, and its proof just involves interchanging the roles of
\mathsf{C}_{j}
and
\mathsf{C}_{j+1}
.
∎

Theorem 4.24
.

Let
B
and
C
be two core blocks of Ariki-Koike algebras with (common
\ell
-charge
\mathbf{r}
and)
\mathbf{mv}(B)=\mathbf{mv}(C)
.
Then
B
and
C
are Scopes equivalent if and only if

\mathbf{Sc}(B)=\mathbf{Sc}(C)
,
in which case
S^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
lying in
B
corresponds to
S^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
lying in
C
if and only if
(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*})^{\sigma_{B}\tau_{B}}=(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{*})^{\sigma_{C}\tau_{C}}
, where
\iota_{\ell,e}((\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{r})^{w_{0}})=(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*};\mathbf{r}^{*}_{B})
and
\iota_{\ell,e}((\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{r})^{w_{0}})=(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{*};\mathbf{r}^{*}_{C})
.

Proof.

If
\mathbf{Sc}(B)=\mathbf{Sc}(C)
, then
B
and
C
are Scopes equivalent to initial core blocks
B_{k}
and
C_{l}
respectively, with
\mathbf{Sc}(B_{k})=\mathbf{Sc}(B)=\mathbf{Sc}(C)=\mathbf{Sc}(C_{l})
by Proposition
4.22
. Since

\mathsf{wt}(B_{k})=|\mathbf{mv}(B_{k})|=|\mathbf{mv}(B)|=|\mathbf{mv}(C)|=|\mathbf{mv}(C_{l})|=\mathsf{wt}(C_{l})

by Proposition
3.13
, it follows from Lemma
4.21
that
B_{k}=C_{l}
. The correspondence between the Specht modules of
B
and
C
then follows from Proposition
4.22
.

For the converse, it suffices to show that if
C=\mathsf{s}_{j}\mathrel{\overset{}{\bullet}}B
, and
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
has no addable node with
(e,\mathbf{r})
-residue
j
for all
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
lying in
B
, then
\mathbf{Sc}(B)=\mathbf{Sc}(C)
.
Let
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
be lying in
B
. Since
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
has no addable node with
(e,\mathbf{r})
-residue
j
,
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{w_{0}}
has no addable node with
(e,\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}})
-residue
j
. Hence
\mathsf{hub}_{j}((\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{r})^{w_{0}})\geq 0
.
Thus
0\leq\mathsf{hub}_{j}((\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{r})^{w_{0}})=\mathsf{hub}_{j}(B)=x^{B}_{j+1}-x^{B}_{\overline{j}}-\delta_{j0}\ell
.
If
x^{B}_{j+1}=x^{B}_{\overline{j}}+\delta_{j0}\ell
, then
B=C
, and the conclusion is trivial.
Thus we may assume that
y^{B}_{j+1}>y^{B}_{\overline{j}}+\delta_{j0}
, or
y^{B}_{j+1}=y^{B}_{\overline{j}}+\delta_{j0}
and
z^{B}_{j+1}>z^{B}_{\overline{j}}
.
If the former holds, then we have
\mathbf{Sc}(B)=\mathbf{Sc}(C)
by Proposition
4.17
.
If the latter holds, then we have
\mathsf{ht}_{I_{B}}(z^{B}_{j+1})\geq z^{B}_{j}
by Proposition
4.23
, so that
\mathbf{Sc}(B)=\mathbf{Sc}(C)
by Proposition
4.17
again.
∎

Proposition 4.25
.

Let
B
be a core block of
\mathcal{H}_{n}
.
For each
a\in\mathbb{Z}/\ell\mathbb{Z}
, let
k_{a}=|\{j\in[1,\,e]\mid z^{B}_{j}=a\}|
.
Let
I_{B}=\{i_{1},\dotsc,i_{m}\}
where
0=i_{1}<i_{2}<\dotsb<i_{m}
(recall that
I_{B}=\{\ell-i\mid\mathrm{mv}_{i}(B)=0\}
). Then the
\mathbf{W}_{e}
-orbit of
B
contains exactly

\prod_{b=1}^{m}\frac{(\sum_{a=i_{b}+1}^{i_{b+1}-1}k_{a})!}{\prod_{a=i_{b}+1}^{i_{b+1}-1}k_{a}!}

Scopes equivalence classes, where
i_{b+1}
is to be read as
\ell
when
b=m
.

Proof.

Let

X=\{(\mathbf{z}^{B})^{\rho}\mid\rho\in\mathfrak{S}_{e},\ j<j^{\prime}\text{ if }z^{B}_{\rho(j)}>z^{B}_{\rho(j^{\prime})}\text{ and }\mathsf{ht}_{I_{B}}(z^{B}_{\rho(j)})\geq z^{B}_{\rho(j^{\prime})}\}.

If
\mathsf{ht}_{I_{B}}(z^{B}_{\rho(j)})=i_{b}
, then
z^{B}_{\rho(j)}>z^{B}_{\rho(j^{\prime})}
and
\mathsf{ht}_{I_{B}}(z^{B}_{\rho(j)})\geq z^{B}_{\rho(j^{\prime})}
if and only if (
z^{B}_{\rho(j)}\in[i_{b}+1,i_{b+1}-1]
and
z^{B}_{\rho(j^{\prime})}\leq i_{b}
) or (
i_{b}=z^{B}_{\rho(j)}>z^{B}_{\rho(j^{\prime})}
).
Thus
X
contains precisely all rearrangements of
\mathbf{z}^{B}
such that the
z^{B}_{j}
’s lying in
[i_{b}+1,\,i_{b+1}-1]
appears before those which are equal to
i_{b}
, which in turn appears before those lying in
[i_{b-1}+1,i_{b}-1]
, for each
b\in[2,\,m]
.
Since there are exactly

\frac{(\sum_{a=i_{b}+1}^{i_{b+1}-1}k_{a})!}{\prod_{a=i_{b}+1}^{i_{b+1}-1}k_{a}!}

distinct ways of arranging the
\mathbf{z}^{B}_{j}
’s lying in
[i_{b}+1,\,i_{b+1}-1]
for each
b\in[1,m]
, we see that

|X|=\prod_{b=1}^{m}\frac{(\sum_{a=i_{b}+1}^{i_{b+1}-1}k_{a})!}{\prod_{a=i_{b}+1}^{i_{b+1}-1}k_{a}!}.

Now, for each
w\in\mathbf{W}_{e}
, let
B_{w}=w\mathrel{\overset{}{\bullet}}B
. Then
\mathbf{z}^{B_{w}}=(\mathbf{z}^{B})^{\overline{w}^{-1}}
by Lemma
4.7
, so that

\mathbf{Sc}(B_{w})=(\mathbf{z}^{B_{w}})^{\sigma_{B_{w}}\tau_{B_{w}}}=(\mathbf{z}^{B})^{\overline{w}^{-1}\sigma_{B_{w}}\tau_{B_{w}}}.

Let
\rho_{w}=\overline{w}^{-1}\sigma_{B_{w}}\tau_{B_{w}}
. Then
\rho_{w}\in\mathfrak{S}_{e}
, and
applying Lemma
4.11
to
\tau_{B_{w}}=\tau^{I_{B_{w}}}_{(\mathbf{z}^{B_{w}})^{\sigma_{B_{w}}}}=\tau^{I_{B}}_{(\mathbf{z}^{B})^{\overline{w}^{-1}\sigma_{B_{w}}}}
, we get
j<j^{\prime}
whenever
z^{B}_{\rho_{w}(j)}>z^{B}_{\rho_{w}(j^{\prime})}
and
\mathsf{ht}_{I_{B}}(z^{B}_{\rho_{w}(j)})\geq z^{B}_{\rho_{w}(j^{\prime})}.

Consequently,
\mathbf{Sc}(B_{w})=(\mathbf{z}^{B})^{\rho_{w}}\in X
.

For the converse, let
(\mathbf{z}^{B})^{\rho}\in X
where
\rho\in\mathfrak{S}_{e}
.
Let
B_{0}
be an initial core block that is Scopes equivalent to
B
.
Then
(\mathbf{z}^{B})^{\rho}=\mathbf{Sc}(B_{0})^{\rho^{\prime}}=(\mathbf{z}^{B_{0}})^{\sigma_{B_{0}}\rho^{\prime}}
for some
\rho^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{S}_{e}
.
Since
w\mapsto\overline{w}
is an isomorphism from
\left<\mathsf{s}_{j}\mid j\in\mathbb{Z}/e\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{j_{0}\}\right>
to
\mathfrak{S}_{e}
for all
j_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}/e\mathbb{Z}
,
let
w^{\prime}\in\left<\mathsf{s}_{j}\mid j\in\mathbb{Z}/e\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{j_{B_{0}}\}\right>
such that
\overline{w^{\prime}}=\sigma_{B_{0}}\rho^{\prime-1}\sigma_{B_{0}}^{-1}
.
Let
B^{\prime}=w^{\prime}\mathrel{\overset{}{\bullet}}B_{0}
.
Then
\mathbf{y}^{B^{\prime}}=w^{\prime}\mathrel{\overset{1}{\circ}}\mathbf{y}^{B_{0}}=\mathbf{y}^{B_{0}}
by Lemma
4.7
and Lemma
4.18
(3) since
w^{\prime}\in\left<\mathsf{s}_{j}\mid j\in\mathbb{Z}/e\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{j_{B_{0}}\}\right>
, so that

\sigma_{B^{\prime}}=\sigma_{\mathbf{y}^{B^{\prime}}}=\sigma_{\mathbf{y}^{B_{0}}}=\sigma_{B_{0}}
.
Thus,

(\mathbf{z}^{B^{\prime}})^{\sigma_{B^{\prime}}}=(\mathbf{z}^{B_{0}})^{\overline{w^{\prime}}^{-1}\sigma_{B^{\prime}}}=(\mathbf{z}^{B_{0}})^{(\sigma_{B_{0}}\rho^{\prime}\sigma_{B_{0}}^{-1})\sigma_{B_{0}}}=(\mathbf{z}^{B_{0}})^{\sigma_{B_{0}}\rho^{\prime}}=(\mathbf{z}^{B})^{\rho}.

As
I_{B^{\prime}}=I_{B_{0}}=I_{B}
by Proposition
3.13
, we have

\tau_{B^{\prime}}=\tau^{I_{B^{\prime}}}_{(\mathbf{z}^{B^{\prime}})^{\sigma_{B^{\prime}}}}=\tau^{I_{B}}_{(\mathbf{z}^{B})^{\rho}}=1_{\mathfrak{S}_{e}}

by Corollary
4.12
(2),
since
(\mathbf{z}^{B})^{\rho}\in X
.
Consequently,

\mathbf{Sc}(B^{\prime})=(\mathbf{z}^{B^{\prime}})^{\sigma_{B^{\prime}}\tau_{B^{\prime}}}=(\mathbf{z}^{B^{\prime}})^{\sigma_{B^{\prime}}}=(\mathbf{z}^{B})^{\rho}.

We have thus shown that
X=\{\mathbf{Sc}(w\mathrel{\overset{}{\bullet}}B)\mid w\in\mathbf{W}_{e}\}
.
By Theorem
4.24
and Proposition
3.13
, each distinct
\mathbf{Sc}(w\mathrel{\overset{}{\bullet}}B)
indexes a distinct Scopes equivalence class in
\mathbf{W}_{e}\mathrel{\overset{}{\bullet}}B
, and so there are exactly
|X|
distinct Scopes equivalence classes in
\mathbf{W}_{e}\mathrel{\overset{}{\bullet}}B
.
∎

Corollary 4.26
.

Let
\ell=2
. All core blocks in a
\mathbf{W}_{e}
-orbit are Scopes equivalent.
Equivalently, all core blocks with the same moving vector (with respect to any
\mathbf{r}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{r}^{\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{2}}\cap\overline{\mathcal{A}}^{2}_{e}
) are Scopes equivalent.

Proof.

Let
B
be a core block.
Then
I_{B}=\{0\}
or
I_{B}=\{0,1\}
.
In both cases, we apply Proposition
4.25
to conclude that the
\mathbf{W}_{e}
-orbit of
B
has only one Scopes equivalence class.
∎

We end this section with a proof that all core blocks of Ariki-Koike algebras are Scopes equivalent to Rouquier blocks.
Rouquier blocks were first defined for the group algebras of the symmetric groups and Schur algebras by Chuang and the third author in
[
CT03
]
.
These blocks are very well-behaved, and they have since been generalised to other algebras such as the group algebras of finite general linear groups, Iwahori-Hecke algebras of type
A
and
q
-Schur algebras.
In
[
Lyl23b
]
, Lyle generalises these blocks to Ariki-Koike algebras.
She defines a block
B
of
\mathcal{H}_{n}
to be Rouquier if and only if
for all
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=(\lambda^{(1)},\dotsc,\lambda^{(\ell)})\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
lying in
B
,
with

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{s}}(\mathsf{quot}_{e}(\beta_{r_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)})))=(s^{\boldsymbol{\lambda},i}_{1},\dotsc,s^{\boldsymbol{\lambda},i}_{e})
for all
i\in[1,\,\ell]
,
then
s^{\boldsymbol{\lambda},i}_{j+1}\geq s^{\boldsymbol{\lambda},i}_{j}+\mathsf{wt}_{e}(\lambda^{(i)})-1
for all
i\in[1,\,\ell]
and
j\in[1,\,e-1]
.

Corollary 4.27

(cf.
[
MNSS24
, Theorem C]
)
.

Every core block of
\mathcal{H}_{n}
is Scopes equivalent to a Rouquier block as defined by Lyle in
[
Lyl23b
]
.

Proof.

Suppose first that
\mathbf{y}^{B}
is strictly increasing, i.e.
y^{B}_{1}<y^{B}_{2}<\dotsb<y^{B}_{e}
.
Let
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=(\lambda^{(1)},\dotsc,\lambda^{(\ell)})\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
be lying in
B
and let

\beta_{\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{w_{0}})=(\mathsf{B}^{(1)},\dotsc,\mathsf{B}^{(\ell)})
.
Let
\mathsf{quot}_{e}(\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{w_{0}}))=(\mathsf{C}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{C}_{e})
and for each
a\in[1,\,\ell]
, let

\mathsf{quot}_{e}(\mathsf{B}^{(a)})=(\mathsf{C}^{(a)}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{C}^{(a)}_{e})
.
Then
\mathcal{s}(\mathsf{C}_{j})=\sum_{a=1}^{\ell}\mathcal{s}(\mathsf{C}^{(a)}_{j})
by
[
LT24
, Lemma 2.6(2)]
.

By Lemma
4.2
(with
i=\ell
),
\mathsf{C}_{j}=\mathbb{Z}_{<y^{B}_{j}\ell}\cup L_{j}
for some
L_{j}\subseteq[y^{B}_{j}\ell,\,y^{B}_{j}\ell+e-1]
for all
j\in[1,\,e]
.
Consequently, for each
a\in[1,\,\ell]
and
j\in[1,\,e]
,

\mathsf{C}^{(a)}_{j}=\mathbb{Z}_{<y^{B}_{j}}\cup M^{a}_{j}

where
M^{a}_{j}=\{y^{B}_{j}\}
if
y^{B}_{j}\ell+(\ell-a)\in L_{j}
and
M^{a}_{j}=\emptyset
otherwise, so that

y^{B}_{j}\leq\mathcal{s}(\mathsf{C}^{(a)}_{j})\leq y^{B}_{j}+1.

Note that if
w_{0}=\sigma\mathbf{t}
where
\mathbf{t}=(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{\ell})\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
and
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{\ell}
, then

\mathsf{B}^{(a)}=\beta_{r_{\sigma(a)}+et_{a}}(\lambda^{(\sigma(a))})=(\beta_{r_{\sigma(a)}}(\lambda^{(\sigma(a)})))^{+et_{a}},

so that

\mathsf{quot}_{e}(\beta_{r_{\sigma(a)}}(\lambda^{(\sigma(a)})))=\mathsf{quot}_{e}((\mathsf{B}^{(a)})^{+(-et_{a})})=((\mathsf{C}^{(a)}_{1})^{+(-t_{a})},\dotsc,(\mathsf{C}^{(a)}_{e})^{+(-t_{a})})

by (
2.1
).
Hence, if
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{s}}(\mathsf{quot}_{e}(\beta_{r_{a}}(\lambda^{(a)}))))=(s^{\boldsymbol{\lambda},a}_{1},\dotsc,s^{\boldsymbol{\lambda},a}_{e})
for
a\in[1,\,\ell]
, then

\displaystyle s^{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\sigma(a)}_{j+1}-s^{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\sigma(a)}_{j}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{s}((\mathsf{C}^{(a)}_{j+1})^{+(-t_{a})})-\mathcal{s}((\mathsf{C}^{(a)}_{j})^{+(-t_{a})})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{s}(\mathsf{C}^{(a)}_{j+1})-\mathcal{s}(\mathsf{C}^{(a)}_{j})\geq y^{B}_{j+1}-y^{B}_{j}-1

\displaystyle\geq 0=\mathsf{wt}_{e}(\lambda^{(\sigma(a))})

for all
a\in[1,\,\ell]
and
j\in[1,\,e-1]
, so that
B
is a Rouquier block.

Now for a general core block
B
, let
\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\dotsc,y_{e})\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
be such that
y_{1}<\dotsb<y_{e}
and
|\mathbf{y}|=|\mathbf{y}^{B}|
.
Then
|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{y}^{B}|=|\mathbf{y}|-|\mathbf{y}^{B}|=0
, so that
\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{y}^{B}\in\mathbf{W}_{e}
by (
2.5
).
Thus

\mathbf{y}=(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{y}^{B})+\mathbf{y}^{B}=(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{y}^{B})\mathrel{\overset{1}{\circ}}\mathbf{y}^{B}\in\mathbf{W}_{e}\mathrel{\overset{1}{\circ}}\mathbf{y}^{B}.

Let
w\in\mathbf{W}_{e}
be the element of least length such that
w\mathrel{\overset{1}{\circ}}\mathbf{y}^{B}=\mathbf{y}
, and
let
\mathsf{s}_{j_{m}}\mathsf{s}_{j_{m-1}}\dotsm\mathsf{s}_{j_{1}}
be a reduced expression for
w
.
Let
B_{0}=B
, and
for each
a\in[1,\,m]
, let
B_{a}=\mathsf{s}_{j_{a}}\mathsf{s}_{j_{a-1}}\dotsm\mathsf{s}_{j_{1}}\mathrel{\overset{}{\bullet}}B
.
Then
\mathbf{y}^{B_{a}}=\mathbf{y}^{\mathsf{s}_{j_{a}}\mathrel{\overset{}{\bullet}}B_{a-1}}=\mathsf{s}_{j_{a}}\mathrel{\overset{1}{\circ}}\mathbf{y}^{B_{a-1}}
by Lemma
4.7
,
so that if
y^{B_{a-1}}_{j_{a}+1}=y^{B_{a-1}}_{\overline{j_{a}}}+\delta_{\overline{j_{a}},e}
,
then
\mathbf{y}^{B_{a}}=\mathbf{y}^{B_{a-1}}
, and hence
\mathsf{s}_{j_{m}}\dotsm\widehat{\mathsf{s}_{j_{a}}}\dotsm\mathsf{s}_{j_{1}}\mathrel{\overset{1}{\circ}}\mathbf{y}^{B}=\mathbf{y}
, contradicting the minimality of the length of
w
.
Thus,
y^{B_{a-1}}_{j_{a}+1}\neq y^{B_{a-1}}_{\overline{j_{a}}}+\delta_{\overline{j_{a}},e}
, so that

\mathsf{B}_{a-1}
and
\mathsf{B}_{a}
are Scopes equivalent by Lemma
4.8
.
Since this is true for all
a\in[1,\,m]
,
B
is Scopes equivalent to
B_{m}
.
As
\mathbf{y}^{B_{m}}=\mathbf{y}^{w\mathrel{\overset{}{\bullet}}B}=w\mathrel{\overset{1}{\circ}}\mathbf{y}^{B}=\mathbf{y}
is strictly increasing,
B_{m}
is Rouquier and our proof is complete.
∎

5.
Simple modules and decomposition numbers of core blocks

Core blocks satisfying Condition (I) of being initial are very special.
In this section, we exploit their properties to express the number of simple modules lying in a core block as a classical Kostka number—the number of semistandard generalised tableaux of a particular shape and a particular type—and relate the graded decomposition numbers of core blocks when
\mathrm{char}(\mathbb{F})=0
with some corresponding graded decomposition numbers for Iwahori-Hecke algebras of type
A
.

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
B
be a core block of
\mathcal{H}_{n}
satisfying Condition (I) of being initial.
Let
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
be lying in
B
with
\beta_{\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{w_{0}})=(\mathsf{B}^{(1)},\dotsc,\mathsf{B}^{(\ell)})
.
For each
a\in[1,\ell]
, we have

\mathrm{min}(\mathbb{Z}\setminus\mathsf{B}^{(a)})\geq|\mathbf{y}^{B}|\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathrm{max}(\mathsf{B}^{(a)})<|\mathbf{y}^{B}|+e.

Consequently,
\mathsf{B}^{(a)}=\mathbb{Z}_{<|\mathbf{y}^{B}|}\cup L_{a}
for some
L_{a}\subseteq[|\mathbf{y}^{B}|,|\mathbf{y}^{B}|+e-1]
with
|L_{a}|=r^{\prime}_{a}-|\mathbf{y}^{B}|
, where
\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}=(r^{\prime}_{1},\dotsc,r^{\prime}_{\ell})
.

Proof.

Set
(\mathsf{C}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{C}_{e}):=\mathsf{quot}_{e}(\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{w_{0}})))=\mathsf{quot}_{e}(\mathbf{U}(\mathsf{B}^{(1)},\dotsc,\mathsf{B}^{(\ell)}))
.
Let
x\in\mathbb{Z}
, and write
x=b_{x}e+r_{x}
, where
b_{x}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
r_{x}\in\mathbb{Z}/e\mathbb{Z}
.
By Lemma
4.2
with
i=\ell
, for each
j\in[1,\,e]
,

x\in\mathsf{C}_{j}
for all
x<y^{B}_{j}\ell
while
x\notin\mathsf{C}_{j}
for all
x\geq(y^{B}_{j}+1)\ell
.

Let
a\in[1,\,\ell]
.

Suppose that
x<|\mathbf{y}^{B}|
.
Then

x<\mathfrak{y}_{B}e+j_{B}
by Definition
4.5
,
so that either

b_{x}\leq\mathfrak{y}_{B}-1

or (
b_{x}=\mathfrak{y}_{B}
and
r_{x}<j_{B}
).
In the former case, we have

\displaystyle b_{x}\ell+\ell-a

\displaystyle\leq(\mathfrak{y}_{B}-1)\ell+\ell-a=(y^{B}_{r_{x}+1}-\mathbbm{1}_{r_{x}+1\leq j_{B}})\ell-a<y^{B}_{r_{x}+1}\ell.

where the equality in the middle follows from Lemma
4.18
(2).
In the latter case, we similarly have

\displaystyle b_{x}\ell+\ell-a

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{y}_{B}\ell+\ell-a=y^{B}_{r_{x}+1}\ell-a<y^{B}_{r_{x}+1}\ell.

Consequently,
b_{x}\ell+\ell-a\in\mathsf{C}_{r_{x}+1}
in all cases, so that

\upsilon_{a}(x)=b_{x}\ell e+(\ell-a)e+r_{x}=(b_{x}\ell+\ell-a)e+r_{x}\in\mathbf{U}(\mathsf{B}^{(1)},\dotsc,\mathsf{B}^{(\ell)})=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}\upsilon_{i}(\mathsf{B}^{(i)}),

and hence
x\in\mathsf{B}^{(a)}
.
Thus
\mathrm{min}(\mathbb{Z}\setminus\mathsf{B}^{(a)})\geq|\mathbf{y}^{B}|
.

Now suppose that
x\geq|\mathbf{y}^{B}|+e
. Then

x\geq(\mathfrak{y}_{B}+1)e+j_{B},

so that either
b_{x}\geq\mathfrak{y}_{B}+2
or (
b_{x}=\mathfrak{y}_{B}+1
and
r_{x}\geq j_{B}
).
In the former case, we have

\displaystyle b_{x}\ell+\ell-a

\displaystyle\geq(\mathfrak{y}_{B}+2)\ell+\ell-a=(y^{B}_{r_{x}+1}+1)\ell+(1-\mathbbm{1}_{r_{x}+1\leq j_{B}})\ell+(\ell-a)\geq(y^{B}_{r_{x}+1}+1)\ell.

In the latter case, we have

\displaystyle b_{x}\ell+\ell-a

\displaystyle=(\mathfrak{y}_{B}+1)\ell+\ell-a=(y^{B}_{r_{x}+1}+1)\ell+(\ell-a)\geq(y^{B}_{r_{x}+1}+1)\ell.

Consequently,
b_{x}\ell+\ell-a\notin\mathsf{C}_{r_{x}+1}
in all cases, so that

\upsilon_{a}(x)=b_{x}\ell e+(\ell-a)e+r_{x}=(b_{x}\ell+\ell-a)e+r_{x}\notin\mathbf{U}(\mathsf{B}^{(1)},\dotsc,\mathsf{B}^{(\ell)})=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}\upsilon_{i}(\mathsf{B}^{(i)})

and hence
x\notin\mathsf{B}^{(a)}
.
Thus
\mathrm{max}(\mathsf{B}^{(a)})<|\mathbf{y}^{B}|+e
as desired.

The last assertion follows from Lemma
2.1
(2).
∎

Corollary 5.2
.

Let
B
be a core block of
\mathcal{H}_{n}
satisfying Condition (I) of being initial.
For each
i\in[1,\,\ell]
, let
t_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}/e\mathbb{Z}
be such that
t_{i}\equiv_{e}r_{i}-|\mathbf{y}^{B}|
.
If
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=(\lambda^{(1)},\dotsc,\lambda^{(\ell)})\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
lies in
B
, then

\beta_{t_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)})=\mathbb{Z}_{<0}\cup L^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{i}

for some
L^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{i}\subseteq[0,e-1]
such that
|L^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{i}|=t_{i}
.

Proof.

Let
w_{0}=\sigma\mathbf{u}
, where
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{\ell}
and
\mathbf{u}=(u_{1},\dotsc,u_{\ell})\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
.
Then
\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}=(r_{\sigma(1)}+eu_{1},\dotsc,r_{\sigma(\ell)}+eu_{\ell})
and
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{w_{0}}=(\lambda^{(\sigma(1))},\dotsc,\lambda^{(\sigma(\ell))})
. Thus, by Proposition
5.1
, for each
a\in[1,\ell]
,

\beta_{r_{\sigma(a)}+eu_{a}}(\lambda^{(\sigma(a))})=\mathbb{Z}_{<|\mathbf{y}^{B}|}\cup L_{a}

for some
L_{a}\subseteq[|\mathbf{y}^{B}|,|\mathbf{y}^{B}|+e-1]
, with
|L_{a}|=r_{\sigma(a)}+eu_{a}-|\mathbf{y}^{B}|
.
Now,

[0,\,e]\ni|L_{a}|=r_{\sigma(a)}+eu_{a}-|\mathbf{y}^{B}|\equiv_{e}t_{\sigma(a)}\in\mathbb{Z}/e\mathbb{Z},

so that
t_{\sigma(a)}=|L_{a}|=r_{\sigma(a)}+eu_{a}-|\mathbf{y}^{B}|
unless
|L_{a}|=e
, in which case
L_{a}=[|\mathbf{y}^{B}|,|\mathbf{y}^{B}|+e-1]
and
t_{\sigma(a)}=r_{\sigma(a)}+eu_{a}-|\mathbf{y}^{B}|-e
.
Define

L^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{\sigma(a)}:=\begin{cases}L_{a}^{+(-|\mathbf{y}^{B}|)},&\text{if }|L_{a}|<e;\\
\emptyset,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Then
L^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{\sigma(a)}\subseteq[0,e-1]
with
|L^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{\sigma(a)}|=t_{\sigma(a)}
, and

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}_{<0}\cup L_{\sigma(a)}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}(\mathbb{Z}_{<|\mathbf{y}^{B}|}\cup L_{a})^{+(-|\mathbf{y}^{B}|)}&\text{if }|L_{a}|<e\\
(\mathbb{Z}_{<|\mathbf{y}^{B}|}\cup L_{a})^{+(-|\mathbf{y}^{B}|-e)}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}(\beta_{r_{\sigma(a)}+eu_{a}-|\mathbf{y}^{B}|}(\lambda^{(\sigma(a))}))&\text{if }|L_{a}|<e\\
(\beta_{r_{\sigma(a)}+eu_{a}-|\mathbf{y}^{B}|-e}(\lambda^{(\sigma(a))}))&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\beta_{t_{\sigma(a)}}(\lambda^{(\sigma(a))}).

∎

Recall that for
(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}\times\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
and
j\in\mathbb{Z}/e\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathsf{AR}^{e,\mathbf{t}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
denotes the set of addable and removable nodes of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
with
(e,\mathbf{t})
-residue
j
, and that for
(a,b,i)\in\mathsf{AR}^{e,\mathbf{t}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
,
\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(a,b,i)=b-a+t_{i}
where
\mathbf{t}=(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{\ell})
.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=(\lambda^{(1)},\dotsc,\lambda^{(\ell)})\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
and let
\mathbf{t}=(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{\ell})\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
.
Suppose that there exists
m\in\mathbb{Z}
such that for each
i\in[1,\,\ell]
,
\beta_{t_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)})=\mathbb{Z}_{<m}\cup L_{i}
for some subset
L_{i}\subseteq[m,m+e-1]
.

(1)

\boldsymbol{\lambda}
has no node with
(e,\mathbf{t})
-residue
\equiv_{e}m
; in particular it has no removable node with
(e,\mathbf{t})
-residue
\equiv_{e}m
.

(2)

For all
j\in\mathbb{Z}/e\mathbb{Z}
with
j\not\equiv_{e}m
and
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathsf{AR}^{e,\mathbf{t}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
,
\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathfrak{n})=m+j_{m}
, where
j_{m}\in\mathbb{Z}/e\mathbb{Z}
with
j_{m}\equiv_{e}j-m
.

Proof.

(1)

If
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
has a node with
(e,\mathbf{t})
-residue
\equiv_{e}m
, then there exist
x,y,z\in\mathbb{Z}
with
x<y\leq z
such that
x\notin\beta_{t_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)})
,
y\equiv_{e}m
and
z\in\beta_{t_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)})
for some
i\in[1,\,\ell]
.
But if
x\notin\beta_{t_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)})
and
z\in\beta_{t_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)})
, then
x\geq m
and
z\leq m+e-1
since
\mathbb{Z}_{<m}\subseteq\beta_{t_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)})\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{<m+e}
, so that for all
y\in[x+1,\,z]\subseteq[m+1,m+e-1]
,
y\not\equiv_{e}m
.

(2)

Let
\mathfrak{n}=(a,b,i)\in\mathsf{AR}^{e,t}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
where
j\not\equiv_{e}m
.
Then exactly one of
\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathfrak{n})
and
\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathfrak{n})-1
lies in
\beta_{t_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)})=\mathbb{Z}_{<m}\cup L_{i}
.
Since
\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathfrak{n})\equiv_{e}j\not\equiv_{e}m
, we must have
j\equiv_{e}\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathfrak{n})\in[m+1,m+e-1]
.
Thus
\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathfrak{n})=m+j_{m}
, where
j_{m}\in[1,\,e-1]
such that
j_{m}\equiv_{e}j-m
.

∎

Given
(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}\times\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
and
j\in\mathbb{Z}/e\mathbb{Z}
, there are two total orders on the set
\mathsf{AR}^{e,\mathbf{t}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
that are of interest:

\displaystyle(a,b,i)\succeq_{\mathbf{t}}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},i^{\prime})\

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\ \mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(a,b,i)>\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},i^{\prime})\ \vee\ (\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(a,b,i)=\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},i^{\prime})\ \wedge\ i\leq i^{\prime});

\displaystyle(a,b,i)\unrhd_{\mathbf{t}}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},i^{\prime})\

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\ i<i^{\prime}\ \vee\ (i=i^{\prime}\ \wedge\ \mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(a,b,i)\geq\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},i^{\prime})).

It is easy to see that if
\mathbf{t}\equiv_{e}\mathbf{u}
, then
\unrhd_{\mathbf{t}}=\unrhd_{\mathbf{u}}
.
Furthermore,
\unrhd_{\mathbf{t}}\ =\ \succeq_{\mathbf{t}}
when
t_{i}\gg t_{i+1}
for all
i\in[1,\,\ell-1]
.
In addition, when
\ell=1
,
\succeq_{t}\ =\ \succeq_{u}\ =\ \unrhd_{u}\ =\ \unrhd_{t}
for all
t,u\in\mathbb{Z}
.

The
j
-signature
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
associated to the order
\geq\ \in\{\succeq_{\mathbf{t}},\unrhd_{\mathbf{t}}\}
is the sequence of
+
and
-
signs obtained
by examining the nodes in
\mathsf{AR}^{e,\mathbf{t}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
in ascending order with respect to
\geq
and writing a
+
(respectively
-
) for
each addable (respectively removable) node. From this, the
reduced
j
-signature
is obtained by successively deleting all adjacent pairs
-+
.
If there are any
-
signs in the reduced
j
-signature, the corresponding removable nodes of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
with
(e,\mathbf{t})
-residue
j
are called
normal
and the smallest among these, with respect to
\geq
, is called
good
.

If by repeatedly removing good nodes (possibly with different
(e,\mathbf{t})
-residues) with respect to
\succeq_{\mathbf{t}}
(resp.
\unrhd_{\mathbf{t}}
) from
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
we reach
\boldsymbol{\varnothing}
, we say that
(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})
is
Uglov
(resp.
Kleshchev
).

Kleshchev
\ell
-partitions play an important role in the representation theory of Ariki-Koike algebras as they index the latter’s simple modules. Indeed,
if
(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{r})
is Kleshchev, then the associated Specht module

S^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
has a simple head
D^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
, and

\{D^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}(n),\,(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{r})\text{ Kleshchev}\}
is a complete set of pairwise non-isomorphic simple
\mathcal{H}_{n}
-modules.

The problem of finding a non-recursive description for all Kleshchev and Uglov partitions is currently still open.
This problem has however been solved for Uglov
\ell
-partitions with
\mathbf{t}\in\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{e}^{\ell}
, now commonly known as FLOTW
\ell
-partitions.
A non-recursive description of such
\ell
-partitions is given in
[
Jac18
]
.

Theorem 5.4

(see
[
Jac18
, Proposition 4.2.1]
)
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=(\lambda^{(1)},\dotsc,\lambda^{(\ell)})\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
and
\mathbf{t}=(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{\ell})\in\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{e}^{\ell}
. Then
(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})
is Uglov if and only if

(1)

\lambda^{(i)}_{a}\geq\lambda^{(i+1)}_{a+t_{i+1}-t_{i}}
for all
i\in[1,\,\ell-1]
and
a\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
;

(2)

\lambda^{(\ell)}_{a}\geq\lambda^{(1)}_{a+e+t_{1}-t_{\ell}}
for all
a\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
;

(3)

\{\mathsf{res}_{e}^{\mathbf{t}}(a,\lambda^{(i)}_{a},i)\mid i\in[1,\,\ell],\ a\in\mathbb{Z}^{+},\,\lambda^{(a)}_{i}=k\}\neq\mathbb{Z}/e\mathbb{Z}
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
.

Since the
a
-th largest element of
\beta_{t_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)})
equals
\lambda^{(i)}_{a}-a+t_{i}=\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(a,\lambda^{(i)}_{a},i)
, we have the following equivalent characterisation of FLOTW partitions in terms of
\beta
-sets, which is more useful for us:

Corollary 5.5
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=(\lambda^{(1)},\dotsc,\lambda^{(\ell)})\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
and
\mathbf{t}=(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{\ell})\in\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{e}^{\ell}
. Then
(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})
is Uglov if and only if

(1)

a
-th largest element of
\beta_{t_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)})\geq

(a+t_{i+1}-t_{i})
-th largest element of
\beta_{t_{i+1}}(\lambda^{(i+1)})
for all
i\in[1,\,\ell-1]
and
a\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
;

(2)

a
-th largest element of
\beta_{t_{\ell}}(\lambda^{(\ell)})\geq e\ +

(a+e+t_{1}-t_{\ell})
-th largest element of
\beta_{t_{1}}(\lambda^{(1)})
for all
a\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
;

(3)

\{\mathsf{res}_{e}^{\mathbf{t}}(a,\lambda^{(i)}_{a},i)\mid i\in[1,\,\ell],\ a\in\mathbb{Z}^{+},\,\lambda^{(a)}_{i}=k\}\neq\mathbb{Z}/e\mathbb{Z}
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
.

For
\eta\in\mathcal{P}
, a
generalised
\eta
-tableau
is a function
\mathsf{T}:[\eta]\to\mathbb{Z}^{+}
. Furthermore,
\mathsf{T}
is
column semistandard
if
\mathsf{T}(i,j)<\mathsf{T}(i,j^{\prime})
and
\mathsf{T}(i,j)\leq\mathsf{T}(i^{\prime},j)
for all
(i,j),(i,j^{\prime}),(i^{\prime},j)\in[\eta]
with
i<i^{\prime}
and
j<j^{\prime}
.
Furthermore, the
type
of
\mathsf{T}
is the composition
(|\mathsf{T}^{-1}\{1\}|,|\mathsf{T}^{-1}\{2\}|,\dotsc)
.

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=(\lambda^{(1)},\dotsc,\lambda^{(\ell)})\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
and let
\mathbf{t}=(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{\ell})\in\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{e}^{\ell}
.
Suppose that there exists
m\in\mathbb{Z}
such that for each
i\in[1,\,\ell]
,
\beta_{t_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)})=\mathbb{Z}_{<m}\cup L_{i}
for some subset
L_{i}\subseteq[m,m+e-1]
.
Let
\mathsf{T}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
denote the generalised
(|L_{\ell}|,|L_{\ell-1}|,\dotsc,|L_{1}|)
-tableau
\mathsf{T}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
, defined by

\mathsf{T}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(i,j)=1-m\ +\text{ $j$-th least element of }L_{\ell-i+1}.

(1)

The type of
\mathsf{T}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
is
(k_{1},\dotsc,k_{e})
where
k_{j}=|\{a\in[1,\,\ell]:m+j-1\in L_{a}\}|
for all
j\in[1,\,e]
.

(2)

(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})
is Uglov if and only if
\mathsf{T}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
is column semistandard.

Proof.

(1)

This is immediate from the definition of
\mathsf{T}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
.

(2)

Note first that
|L_{i}|-|L_{i^{\prime}}|=t_{i}-t_{i^{\prime}}
for all
i,i^{\prime}\in[1,\,\ell]
by Lemma
2.1
(2).

For each
i\in[1,\,\ell]
, the
a
-th largest element of
\beta_{t_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)})
is the
a
-th largest element of
L_{i}
if
a\leq|L_{i}|
, and equals
m-a+|L_{i}|
if
a>|L_{i}|
.
Now
a>|L_{i}|
if and only if

a+t_{i+1}-t_{i}=a+|L_{i+1}|-|L_{i}|>|L_{i+1}|,

so that when these hold, we have

a
-th largest element of
\beta_{t_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)})

\displaystyle=m-a+|L_{i}|=m-(a-t_{i}+t_{i+1})+|L_{i+1}|

\displaystyle=\text{$(a+t_{i+1}-t_{i})$-th largest element of $\beta_{t_{i+1}}(\lambda^{(i+1)})$}.

Thus Condition (1) in Corollary
5.5
is reduced to: for all
i\in[1,\,\ell-1]

\text{$a$-th largest element of $L_{i}$}\ \geq\ \text{$(a+t_{i+1}-t_{i})$-th largest element of $L_{i+1}$}

for all
a\in[1,\,|L_{i}|]
,
which is equivalent to

\mathsf{T}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\ell-i+1,|L_{i}|+1-a)\geq\mathsf{T}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\ell-i,|L_{i}|+1-a)

for all
a\in[1,\,|L_{i}|]
.
Replacing
i
and
a
with
\ell-i
and
|L_{i}|+1-a
respectively, the condition becomes

\mathsf{T}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(i,a)\leq\mathsf{T}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(i+1,a)

for all
i\in[1,\,\ell-1]
and
a\in[1,|L_{i}|]
.

Since
a+e+t_{1}-t_{\ell}=a+e+|L_{1}|-|L_{\ell}|>|L_{1}|
,

\displaystyle(a+e+t_{1}-t_{\ell})

\displaystyle\text{-th largest element of $\beta_{t_{1}}^{(\lambda^{(1)})}$}\ =m-a-e+|L_{\ell}|

\displaystyle=\ \text{$(e+a)$-th largest element of $\beta_{t_{\ell}}(\lambda^{(\ell)})$}

\displaystyle\leq\text{$a$-th largest element of $\beta_{t_{\ell}}(\lambda^{(\ell)})$}-e.

Hence Condition (2) in Corollary
5.5
holds for
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
.

Condition (3) in Corollary
5.5
also holds for
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
, since
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
has no node with
(e,\mathbf{t})
-residue
\equiv_{e}m
by Lemma
5.3
(1).

Thus,
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
is Uglov if and only if
\mathsf{T}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(i,a)\leq\mathsf{T}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(i+1,a)
for all
i\in[1,\,\ell-1]
and
a\in[1,\,|L_{i}|]
, if and only if
\mathsf{T}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
is column semistandard, since
\mathsf{T}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(i,b)<\mathsf{T}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(i,b+1)
for all
i\in[1,\,\ell]
and
b\in[1,\,|L_{i}|-1]
follows from the definition of
\mathsf{T}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
.

∎

Recall that
\mathsf{AR}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
denotes the set of addable and removable nodes of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
for all
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=(\lambda^{(1)},\dotsc,\lambda^{(\ell)})\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
and

\mathbf{t}=(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{\ell})\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
.
Suppose that there exists
m\in\mathbb{Z}
such that for each
i\in[1,\,\ell]
,
\beta_{t_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)})=\mathbb{Z}_{<m}\cup L_{i}
for some
L_{i}\subseteq[m,\,m+e+1]
.

(1)

For all
\mathfrak{n}=(a,b,i),\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}=(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},i^{\prime})\in\mathsf{AR}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
with
\mathsf{res}^{\mathbf{t}}_{e}(\mathfrak{n})=\mathsf{res}^{\mathbf{t}}_{e}(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})\not\equiv_{e}m
,

\mathfrak{n}\succeq_{\mathbf{t}}\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\ \Leftrightarrow\ i\leq i^{\prime}\ \Leftrightarrow\ \mathfrak{n}\unrhd_{\mathbf{t}}\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}.

(2)

(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})
is Kleshchev if and only if
(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})
is Uglov.

Proof.

(1)

By Lemma
5.3
(2),
\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathfrak{n})=\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})
if and only if
\mathsf{res}_{e}^{\mathbf{t}}(\mathfrak{n})=\mathsf{res}^{\mathbf{t}}_{e}(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})
.
Consequently,

\mathfrak{n}\succeq_{\mathbf{t}}\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\ \Leftrightarrow\ i\leq i^{\prime}\ \Leftrightarrow\ \mathfrak{n}\unrhd_{\mathbf{t}}\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}.

(2)

We prove by induction on
|\boldsymbol{\lambda}|
.
By Lemma
5.3
(1) and part (1), a removable node of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
is good with respect to
\succeq_{\mathbf{t}}
if and only if it is good with respect to
\unrhd_{\mathbf{t}}
.
Consequently,
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
has no good node with respect to
\succeq_{\mathbf{t}}
if and only if
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
has no good node with respect to
\unrhd_{\mathbf{t}}
, in which case
(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})
is neither Kleshchev nor Uglov.
Otherwise, let
\mathfrak{n}=(a,b,i)
be a good node of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
with respect to both
\unrhd_{\mathbf{t}}
and
\succeq_{\mathbf{t}}
, and let

\boldsymbol{\mu}=(\mu^{(1)},\dotsc,\mu^{(\ell)}):=\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{-\mathfrak{n}}
.
Then

\displaystyle\beta_{t_{i}}(\mu^{(i)})

\displaystyle=\beta_{t_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)})\setminus\{\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(a,b,i)\}\cup\{\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(a,b,i)-1\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{Z}_{<m}\cup L_{i}\setminus\{\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(a,b,i)\}\cup\{\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(a,b,i)-1\}=\mathbb{Z}_{<m}\cup M_{i}

where
M_{i}=L_{i}\setminus\{\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(a,b,i)\}\cup\{\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(a,b,i)-1\}\subseteq[m,m+e-1]
,
while
\beta_{t_{i^{\prime}}}(\mu^{(i^{\prime})})=\beta_{t_{i^{\prime}}}(\lambda^{(i^{\prime})})=\mathbb{Z}_{<m}\cup L_{i^{\prime}}
for all
i^{\prime}\in[1,\,\ell]
with
i^{\prime}\neq i
.
By induction,
(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{t})
is Kleshchev if and only if
(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{t})
is Uglov.
Thus,
(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})
is Kleshchev if and only if
(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})
is Uglov.

∎

For
\eta\in\mathcal{P}
, its conjugate
\eta^{\prime}
is the partition with
[\eta^{\prime}]=\{(j,i)\mid(i,j)\in[\eta]\}
.
A generalised
\eta
-tableau
\mathsf{T}
is
semistandard
if and only if
\mathsf{T}^{\prime}
, defined by
\mathsf{T}^{\prime}(j,i)=\mathsf{T}(i,j)
, is a column semistandard generalised
\eta^{\prime}
-tableau.
Semistandard generalised tableaux appears as composition multiplicities of permutation modules of symmetric groups.
More precisely, the number of semistandard generalised
\eta
-tableaux of type
\zeta
equals the composition multiplicity of the Specht module
S^{\eta}
in the permutation module
M^{\zeta}
in characteristic zero
[
Jam78
, Theorem 14.1]
.
If
\tilde{\zeta}
is a composition obtained by rearranging the parts of
\zeta
, then it is well known that
M^{\zeta}
and
M^{\tilde{\zeta}}
are isomorphic,
so that the composition multiplicities of
S^{\eta}
in
M^{\zeta}
and in
M^{\tilde{\zeta}}
are the same.
Consequently, the number of semistandard generalised
\eta
-tableaux of type
\zeta
equals that of type
\tilde{\zeta}
.
Since the semistandard generalised
\eta
-tableaux are in a type-preserving bijective correspondence with the column semistandard generalised
\eta^{\prime}
-tableaux,
we have that the number of column semistandard generalised
\eta
-tableaux of type
\zeta
equals that of type
\tilde{\zeta}
too.
We shall use this result freely in the proof of the following theorem.

Theorem 5.8
.

Let
B
be a core block of
\mathcal{H}_{n}
, and write
\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}=(r^{\prime}_{1},\dotsc,r^{\prime}_{\ell})
.
Then the number of simple
\mathcal{H}_{n}
-modules lying in
B
equals the number of generalised column semistandard
\eta^{B}
-tableaux of type
\mathbf{z}^{B}
, where

\eta^{B}=(\eta^{B}_{1},\dotsc,\eta^{B}_{\ell})\in\mathcal{P}
with

\eta^{B}_{i}=r^{\prime}_{\ell+1-i}-|\mathbf{y}^{B}|

for all
i\in[1,\,\ell]
.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{F},q,\mathbf{r}}(n)=\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{F},q,\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}}(n)
, by replacing
\mathbf{r}
with
\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}
, we may assume that
w_{0}=1_{\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{\ell}}
.

We first assume that
B
is an initial core block.
For each
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=(\lambda^{(1)},\dotsc,\lambda^{(\ell)})\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
lying in
B
with
\beta_{\mathbf{r}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})=(\mathsf{B}^{(1)},\dotsc,\mathsf{B}^{(\ell)})
, and each
i\in[1,\,\ell]
, we have

\mathsf{B}^{(i)}=\mathbb{Z}_{<|\mathbf{y}^{B}|}\cup L_{i}

for some
L_{i}\subseteq[|\mathbf{y}^{B}|,\,|\mathbf{y}^{B}|+e-1]
with
|L_{i}|=r^{\prime}_{i}-|\mathbf{y}^{B}|=\eta_{\ell+1-i}
by Proposition
5.1
.
If

\mathsf{quot}_{e}(\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{r}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})))=(\mathsf{C}_{1},\dotsc,\mathsf{C}_{e})
, then for each
j\in[1,\,e]
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{j}

\displaystyle=\left\{\frac{x-j+1}{e}\ \bigg|\ x\in\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{r}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})),\,x\equiv_{e}j-1\right\}=\left\{\frac{x-j+1}{e}\ \bigg|\ x\in\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}\upsilon_{i}(\mathsf{B}^{(i)}),\,x\equiv_{e}j-1\right\}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}\left\{\frac{x-j+1}{e}\ \bigg|\ x\in\upsilon_{i}(\mathbb{Z}_{<|\mathbf{y}^{B}|}\cup L_{i}),\,x\equiv_{e}j-1\right\}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}\left\{\frac{\upsilon_{i}(x^{\prime})-j+1}{e}\ \bigg|\ x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{<|\mathbf{y}^{B}|}\cup L_{i},\,x^{\prime}\equiv_{e}j-1\right\}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}\left(\left\{a\ell+\ell-i\ \bigg|\ a\in\mathbb{Z},\,ae+j-1<|\mathbf{y}^{B}|\right\}\cup\left\{\frac{\upsilon_{i}(x^{\prime})-j+1}{e}\ \bigg|\ x^{\prime}\in L_{i},\,x^{\prime}\equiv_{e}j-1\right\}\right).

Since
|\mathbf{y}^{B}|=\mathfrak{y}_{B}e+j_{B}
by Definition
4.5
, we see that
the necessary and sufficient condition for
ae+j-1<|\mathbf{y}^{B}|
is
a\leq\mathfrak{y}_{B}
when
j-1<j_{B}
, and
a\leq\mathfrak{y}_{B}-1
when
j-1\geq j_{B}
.
Consequently, when
j\leq j_{B}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{j}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}\left(\left\{a\ell+\ell-i\ \bigg|\ a\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq\mathfrak{y}_{B}}\right\}\cup\left\{\frac{\upsilon_{i}(x^{\prime})-j+1}{e}\ \bigg|\ x^{\prime}\in L_{i},\,x^{\prime}\equiv_{e}j-1\right\}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{Z}_{<(\mathfrak{y}_{B}+1)\ell}\cup\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}\left\{\frac{\upsilon_{i}(x^{\prime})-j+1}{e}\ \bigg|\ x^{\prime}\in L_{i},\,x^{\prime}\equiv_{e}j-1\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{Z}_{<y^{B}_{j}\ell}\cup\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}\left\{\frac{\upsilon_{i}(x^{\prime})-j+1}{e}\ \bigg|\ x^{\prime}\in L_{i},\,x^{\prime}\equiv_{e}j-1\right\}

(5.1)

by Lemma
4.18
(2).
On the other hand, when
j>j_{B}
, we similarly have

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{j}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}\left(\left\{a\ell+\ell-i\ \bigg|\ a\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq\mathfrak{y}_{B}-1}\right\}\cup\left\{\frac{\upsilon_{i}(x^{\prime})-j+1}{e}\ \bigg|\ x^{\prime}\in L_{i},\,x^{\prime}\equiv_{e}j-1\right\}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{Z}_{<\mathfrak{y}_{B}\ell}\cup\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}\left\{\frac{\upsilon_{i}(x^{\prime})-j+1}{e}\ \bigg|\ x^{\prime}\in L_{i},\,x^{\prime}\equiv_{e}j-1\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{Z}_{<y^{B}_{j}\ell}\cup\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}\left\{\frac{\upsilon_{i}(x^{\prime})-j+1}{e}\ \bigg|\ x^{\prime}\in L_{i},\,x^{\prime}\equiv_{e}j-1\right\}.

(5.2)

From (
5.1
) and (
5.2
), since the unions there are disjoint unions, we get

\displaystyle y^{B}_{j}\ell+z^{B}_{j}=x^{B}_{j}=\mathcal{s}(\mathsf{C}_{j})

\displaystyle=y^{B}_{j}\ell+\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}|\{x^{\prime}\in L_{i}\mid x^{\prime}\equiv_{e}j-1\}|,

so that

z^{B}_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}|\{x^{\prime}\in L_{i}\mid x^{\prime}\equiv_{e}j-1\}|=|\{i\in[1,\ell]\mid|\mathbf{y}^{B}|+\tilde{j}\in L_{i}\}|,

where
\tilde{j}\in\mathbb{Z}/e\mathbb{Z}
satisfies
|\mathbf{y}^{B}|+\tilde{j}\equiv_{e}j-1
.
Consequently,
\mathsf{T}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
defined in Proposition
5.6
is a generalised
\eta
-tableau of type

(z^{B}_{j_{B}+1},z^{B}_{j_{B}+2}\dotsc,z^{B}_{e},z^{B}_{1},\dotsc,z^{B}_{j_{B}})=(\mathbf{z}^{B})^{\sigma_{B}}
by Proposition
5.6
(1) and Corollary
4.19
.
By Lemma
5.7
and Proposition
5.6
(2),
(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{r})
is Kleshchev if and only if
\mathsf{T}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
is column semistandard.
Since
\eta
depends only on
\mathbf{r}
and
|\mathbf{y}^{B}|
, and is independent of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
,
the number of simple modules lying in
B
equals the number of column semistandard generalised
\eta
-tableaux of type

(\mathbf{z}^{B})^{\sigma_{B}}
and hence of type
\mathbf{z}^{B}
.

For a general core block
B
, let
B_{0}
be the initial core block that is Scopes equivalent to
B
which exists by Proposition
4.22
and is unique by Lemma
4.21
.
Then
B=w\mathrel{\overset{}{\bullet}}B_{0}
for some
w\in\mathbf{W}_{e}
, and
B
and
B_{0}
has the same number of simple modules.
By Lemma
4.7
, we have

\displaystyle|\mathbf{y}^{B}|=|\mathbf{y}^{B_{0}}|\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathbf{z}^{B}=(\mathbf{z}^{B_{0}})^{\overline{{w}^{-1}}}.

In particular,
\eta^{B}=\eta^{B_{0}}
.
Since the number of column semistandard generalised
\eta^{B}
-tablaux of type
\mathbf{z}^{B}
equals that of type
(\mathbf{z}^{B})^{\sigma}
for any
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{e}
, the Theorem follows.
∎

Corollary 5.9

(cf.
[
Lyl23a
, Theorem 3.20(2)]
)
.

Let
\ell=2
, and let
B
be a core block of
\mathcal{H}_{n}
. Then the number of simple modules lying in
B
equals

\binom{|\mathbf{z}^{B}|}{r^{\prime}_{1}-|\mathbf{y}^{B}|}-\binom{|\mathbf{z}^{B}|}{r^{\prime}_{1}-|\mathbf{y}^{B}|-1},

where
\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}=(r^{\prime}_{1},r^{\prime}_{2})
.

Proof.

Let
a=r^{\prime}_{1}-|\mathbf{y}^{B}|
and
b=r^{\prime}_{2}-|\mathbf{y}^{B}|
.
By Theorem
5.8
, the number of simple modules lying in
B
equals the number of column semistandard generalised
(b,a)
-tableaux of type
\mathbf{z}^{B}
.
Since
\ell=2
,
z^{B}_{j}\in\{0,1\}
for all
j\in[1,\,e]
. Consequently,
a generalised
(b,a)
-tableau of type
\mathbf{z}^{B}
is column semistandard if and only if it is a standard
(b,a)
-tableau.
It is well known that the number of standard
(b,a)
-tableaux is equal to the dimension of the Specht module
S^{(b,a)}
of the symmetric group (or Iwahori-Hecke algebra of type
A
), which is known to be
\binom{a+b}{a}-\binom{a+b}{a-1}
(see, for example,
[
Jam78
, Example 14.4]
).
To complete the proof, we only need to observe that
(b,a)
and
\mathbf{z}^{B}
have the same size (so that we can talk about
(b,a)
-tableaux of type
\mathbf{z}^{B}
).
∎

The final main result of this paper is to connect the graded decomposition numbers of the core blocks of Ariki-Koike algebras with those of Iwahori-Hecke algebras of type
A
, when
\mathrm{char}(\mathbb{F})=0
.
We do this through the
v
-decomposition numbers arising from the canonical bases of the Fock space representations of the quantum affine algebra
U_{v}(\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{e})
, which we now proceed to give a brief account.

Let
v
be an indeterminate. The quantum affine algebra
U_{v}(\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{e})
is a unital
\mathbb{C}(v)
-algebra generated by
\{e_{j},f_{j},k_{j},k_{j}^{-1}\mid j\in\mathbb{Z}/e\mathbb{Z}\}\cup\{D,D^{-1}\}
subject to certain relations which we do not
need here (interested readers may refer to
[
Lec02
, Section 4]
for more details).
The level
\ell
Fock space representation
\mathcal{F}^{\ell}
of
U_{v}(\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{e})
is a
\mathbb{C}(v)
-vector space with
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}\times\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
as its
\mathbb{C}(v)
-basis.
For each
\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
, the
\mathbb{C}(v)
-subspace
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{t}}
spanned by
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}\times\{\mathbf{t}\}
is invariant under the
U_{v}(\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{e})
-action, so that
\mathcal{F}^{\ell}
decomposes naturally into a direct sum of the
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{t}}
’s, i.e.
\mathcal{F}^{\ell}=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}}\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{t}}
.
For our purposes, we require only the precise description of the action of the generator
f_{j}
of
U_{v}(\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{e})
on
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{t}}
, which is

f_{j}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})=\sum_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathsf{A}^{e,\mathbf{t}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})}v^{N_{\mathbf{t}}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\mathfrak{n})}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{+\mathfrak{n}};\mathbf{t})

where
N_{\mathbf{t}}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\mathfrak{n})=|\{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{A}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\mid\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\rhd_{\mathbf{t}}\mathfrak{n}\}|-|\{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{R}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\mid\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\rhd_{\mathbf{t}}\mathfrak{n}\}|.
(Recall that
\mathsf{A}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
(resp.
\mathsf{R}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
) denotes the set of addable (resp. removable) nodes of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
, and
\mathsf{A}^{e,\mathbf{t}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
is the subset of
\mathsf{A}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
consisting of nodes with
(e,\mathbf{t})
-residue
j
.)

Proposition 5.10
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=(\lambda^{(1)},\dotsc,\lambda^{(\ell)})\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
and

\mathbf{t}=(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{\ell})\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
.
Suppose that there exists
m\in\mathbb{Z}
such that for each
i\in[1,\,\ell]
,
\beta_{t_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)})=\mathbb{Z}_{<m}\cup L_{i}
for some
L_{i}\subseteq[m,\,m+e+1]
.
Let
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{t}-m\boldsymbol{1}
, and for each
\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
, let
\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\nu}):=\beta^{-1}(\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\nu})))
.
Let
j\in\mathbb{Z}/e\mathbb{Z}
with
j\not\equiv_{e}m
, and let
j_{m}\in\mathbb{Z}/e\mathbb{Z}
with
j_{m}\equiv_{e}j-m
.

(1)

There is a bijective function
\phi:\mathsf{AR}^{e,\mathbf{t}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\to\mathsf{AR}^{e,|\mathbf{u}|}_{j_{m}}(\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))
with the following properties for all
\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{AR}^{e,\mathbf{t}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
:

(a)

\upsilon_{i}(\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{u}}(\mathfrak{n}))=\mathsf{cont}_{|\mathbf{u}|}(\phi(\mathfrak{n}))
if
\mathfrak{n}=(a,b,i)
;

(b)

\mathfrak{n}
is addable if and only if
\phi(\mathfrak{n})
is addable, in which case
\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{+\mathfrak{n}})=(\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))^{+\phi(\mathfrak{n})}
;

(c)

\mathfrak{n}
is removable if and only if
\phi(\mathfrak{n})
is removable, in which case
\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{-\mathfrak{n}})=(\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))^{-\phi(\mathfrak{n})}
;

(d)

\mathfrak{n}\unrhd_{\mathbf{t}}\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
if and only if
\phi(\mathfrak{n})\unrhd_{|\mathbf{u}|}\phi(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})
.

(2)

Define
\Psi_{\mathbf{t},\mathbf{u}}:\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{t}}\to\mathcal{F}_{|\mathbf{u}|}
by
\Psi_{\mathbf{t},\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{t})=(\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\mu});|\mathbf{u}|)
for all
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
and extending it linearly. Then

\displaystyle\Psi_{\mathbf{t},\mathbf{u}}(f_{j}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t}))=f_{j_{m}}(\Psi_{\mathbf{t},\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t}))=f_{j_{m}}(\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda});|\mathbf{u}|).

Proof.

(1)

Since
\mathbf{t}=\mathbf{u}+m\boldsymbol{1}
, we have

\mathsf{res}^{\mathbf{t}}_{e}(a,b,i)\equiv_{e}\mathsf{res}^{\mathbf{u}}_{e}(a,b,i)+m,

so that
\mathsf{AR}^{e,\mathbf{t}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})=\mathsf{AR}^{e,\mathbf{u}}_{j_{m}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
.
Consequently, Lemma
2.4
provides the bijection
\phi
satisfying (a)–(c).

For part (d), let
\mathfrak{n}=(a,b,i),\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}=(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},i^{\prime})\in\mathsf{AR}^{e,\mathbf{t}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
.
By Lemma
5.3
(2),
\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathfrak{n})=\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})
, so that

\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{u}}(\mathfrak{n})=\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{u}}(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})
since
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{t}-m\boldsymbol{1}
.
If
\mathfrak{n}\unrhd_{\mathbf{t}}\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
, then

i\leq i^{\prime}
by Lemma
5.7
(1) .
Consequently,

\mathsf{cont}_{|\mathbf{u}|}(\phi(\mathfrak{n}))=\upsilon_{i}(\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{u}}(\mathfrak{n}))=\upsilon_{i}(\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{u}}(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}))\geq\upsilon_{i^{\prime}}(\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{u}}(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}))=\mathsf{cont}_{|\mathbf{u}|}(\phi(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})),

so that
\phi(\mathfrak{n})\unrhd_{|\mathbf{u}|}\phi(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})
.
Conversely, if
\phi(\mathfrak{n})\unrhd_{|\mathbf{u}|}\phi(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})
, then

\mathsf{cont}_{|\mathbf{u}|}(\phi(\mathfrak{n}))\geq\mathsf{cont}_{|\mathbf{u}|}(\phi(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}))
.
Thus

\upsilon_{i}(\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{u}}(\mathfrak{n}))=\mathsf{cont}_{|\mathbf{u}|}(\phi(\mathfrak{n}))\geq\mathsf{cont}_{|\mathbf{u}|}(\phi(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}))=\upsilon_{i^{\prime}}(\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{u}}(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}))=\upsilon_{i^{\prime}}(\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{u}}(\mathfrak{n}))

so that
i\leq i^{\prime}
and hence
\mathfrak{n}\unrhd_{\mathbf{t}}\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
by Lemme
5.7
(1).

(2)

For
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathsf{A}^{e,\mathbf{t}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
, we have by part (1),

\displaystyle N^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{\mathbf{t}}(\mathfrak{n})

\displaystyle=|\{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{A}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\mid\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\rhd_{\mathbf{t}}\mathfrak{n}\}|-|\{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{R}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\mid\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\rhd_{\mathbf{t}}\mathfrak{n}\}|

\displaystyle=|\{\phi(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})\in\mathsf{A}(\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))\mid\phi(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})\rhd_{|\mathbf{u}|}\phi(\mathfrak{n})\}|-|\{\phi(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})\in\mathsf{R}(\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))\mid\phi(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})\rhd_{|\mathbf{u}|}\phi(\mathfrak{n})\}|

\displaystyle=|\{\mathfrak{m}\in\mathsf{A}(\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))\mid\mathfrak{m}\rhd_{|\mathbf{u}|}\phi(\mathfrak{n})\}|-|\{\mathfrak{m}\in\mathsf{R}(\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))\mid\mathfrak{m}\rhd_{|\mathbf{u}|}\phi(\mathfrak{n})\}|

\displaystyle=N^{\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})}_{|\mathbf{u}|}(\phi(\mathfrak{n})).

Thus,

\displaystyle\Psi_{\mathbf{t},\mathbf{u}}(f_{j}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t}))

\displaystyle=\Psi_{\mathbf{t},\mathbf{u}}\left(\sum_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathsf{A}^{e,\mathbf{t}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})}v^{N_{\mathbf{t}}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\mathfrak{n})}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{+\mathfrak{n}};\mathbf{t})\right)=\sum_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathsf{A}^{e,\mathbf{t}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})}v^{N_{\mathbf{t}}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\mathfrak{n})}(\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{+\mathfrak{n}});|\mathbf{u}|)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\phi(\mathfrak{n})\in\mathsf{A}^{e,|\mathbf{u}|}_{j_{m}}(\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))}v^{N_{|\mathbf{u}|}^{\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})}(\phi(\mathfrak{n}))}((\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))^{+\mathfrak{\phi(n)}};|\mathbf{u}|)

\displaystyle=f_{j_{m}}(\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda});|\mathbf{u}|)=f_{j_{m}}(\Psi_{\mathbf{t},\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})).

∎

The Fock space possesses a bar involution
x\mapsto\overline{x}
(see for example
[
BK09
, Subsection 3.12]
) which satisfies in particular

\overline{a(v)x+y}=a(v^{-1})\overline{x}+\overline{y}
and
\overline{f_{j}(x)}=f_{j}\overline{x}
for all
a(v)\in\mathbb{C}(v)
,
x,y\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{t}}
and
j\in\mathbb{Z}/e\mathbb{Z}
.
There is a distinguished basis
\{G(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t}):\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}\}
for
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{t}}
, called the
canonical basis
, which has the following characterising properties:

•

\overline{G(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})}=G(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})
;

•

G(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})-(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t})\in\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}}v\mathbb{Z}[v](\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{t})
.

For
\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
and
\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
, define
d_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\mathbf{t}}(v)\in\mathbb{C}(v)
by

G(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{t})=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}}d_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\mathbf{t}}(v)(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{t}).

These
v
-decomposition numbers

d_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\mathbf{t}}(v)
enjoy the following remarkable properties.

(1)

[
BK09
, Remark 3.19]

d^{\mathbf{t}}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\boldsymbol{\mu}}(v)=1
and
d^{\mathbf{t}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\boldsymbol{\mu}}(v)\in v\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}[v]
when
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\neq\boldsymbol{\mu}
.

(2)

[
Ari96
]
When
(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{r})
is Kleshchev, then
d^{\mathbf{r}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\boldsymbol{\mu}}(1)
equals the composition multiplicity of
D^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
in the Specht module
S^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
of
\mathcal{H}_{n}
in characteristic
0
. (In fact, more is true: the
v
-decomposition numbers are the graded decomposition numbers of the Ariki-Koike algebras
[
BK09
, Corollary 5.15]
.)

In particular, if
d^{\mathbf{r}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\boldsymbol{\mu}}(v)\neq 0
, then
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
and
\boldsymbol{\mu}
lies in the same block of
\mathcal{H}_{n}
and
\boldsymbol{\mu}\unrhd\boldsymbol{\lambda}
.
(Here,
\boldsymbol{\mu}=(\mu^{(1)},\dotsc,\mu^{(\ell)})\unrhd\boldsymbol{\lambda}=(\lambda^{(1)},\dotsc,\lambda^{(\ell)})
if and only if

\sum_{a=1}^{i-1}|\mu^{(a)}|+\sum_{b=1}^{j}\mu^{(i)}_{b}\geq\sum_{a=1}^{i-1}|\lambda^{(a)}|+\sum_{b=1}^{j}\lambda^{(i)}_{b}

for all
i\in[1,\,\ell]
and
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
.)

We note that
d^{\mathbf{t}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\boldsymbol{\mu}}(v)=d^{\mathbf{u}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\boldsymbol{\mu}}(v)
when
\mathbf{t}\equiv_{e}\mathbf{u}
.
Furthermore, when
\ell=1
,

d^{t}_{\lambda\mu}(v)=d^{u}_{\lambda\mu}(v)
for all
t,u\in\mathbb{Z}
, so that
we may write
d_{\lambda\mu}(v)
without ambiguity.

Proposition 5.11
.

Let
B
be a core block of
\mathcal{H}_{n}
, and suppose that
w_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
and that
B
satisfies Condition (I) of being initial.
Let
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}-|\mathbf{y}^{B}|\boldsymbol{1}
, and for each
\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
, let
\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\nu})=\beta^{-1}(\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\nu})))
. Let
\Psi_{\mathbf{r},\mathbf{u}}:\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{r}}\to\mathcal{F}_{|\mathbf{u}|}
be the
\mathbb{C}(v)
-linear map with
\Psi_{\mathbf{r},\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{r})=(\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda});|\mathbf{u}|)
for all
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
.
If
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is an
\ell
-partition lying in
B
such that
(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{r})
is Kleshchev,
then
\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\mu})
is
e
-regular and

\Psi_{\mathbf{r},\mathbf{u}}(G(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{r}))=G(\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\mu});|\mathbf{u}|).

In particular
d^{\mathbf{r}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\boldsymbol{\mu}}(v)=d_{\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\mu})}(v)

for all
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
, and
d_{\lambda,\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\mu})}(v)=0
if
\lambda\notin\Psi_{\mathbf{u}}(\mathcal{P}^{\ell})
.

Proof.

Since
w_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
, we have
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{w_{0}}=\boldsymbol{\lambda}
for all
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
, and
\mathbf{r}\equiv_{e}\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}
, so that
\unrhd_{\mathbf{r}}\ =\ \unrhd_{\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}}
on
\mathsf{AR}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
for all
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
. In particular,
(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{r})
is Kleshchev if and only if
(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}})
is Kleshchev.
Furthermore,
d^{\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\boldsymbol{\mu}}(v)=d^{\mathbf{r}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\boldsymbol{\mu}}(v)
for all
\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
. As such, it suffices to prove the Theorem with
\mathbf{r}
replaced by
\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}
.

We prove by induction.
If
|\boldsymbol{\mu}|=0
, then
\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{\varnothing}
.
Thus
(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}})
is Kleshchev and
G(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}})=(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}})
.
Since
\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}\in\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{e}^{\ell}
,
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}-|\mathbf{y}^{B}|\boldsymbol{1}\in\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{e}^{\ell}
, and so
\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\mu})=\beta^{-1}(\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\varnothing})))

is an
e
-core partition by
[
JL21
, Proposition 2.22]
.
Hence
\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\mu})
is
e
-regular, and
G(\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\mu});|\mathbf{u}|)=(\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\mu});|\mathbf{u}|)
(see, for example,
[
Lec02
, Proposition 11(i)]
).
Thus

\Psi_{\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}},\mathbf{u}}(G(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}))=\Psi_{\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}},\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}})=(\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\mu});|\mathbf{u}|)=G(\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\mu});|\mathbf{u}|).

Now suppose that
(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}})
is Kleshchev and
|\boldsymbol{\mu}|>0
,
and assume that the statement holds for all
\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
with
|\boldsymbol{\nu}|<|\boldsymbol{\mu}|
, or
|\boldsymbol{\nu}|=|\boldsymbol{\mu}|
and
\boldsymbol{\nu}\lhd\boldsymbol{\mu}
.
By Proposition
5.1
, for each
i\in[1,\,\ell]
,

\beta_{r^{\prime}_{i}}(\mu^{(i)})=\mathbb{Z}_{<|\mathbf{y}^{B}|}\cup M_{i}
for some

M_{i}\subseteq[|\mathbf{y}^{B}|,\,|\mathbf{y}^{B}|+e-1]
, where
\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}=(r^{\prime}_{1},\dotsc,r^{\prime}_{\ell})
.
Since
(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}})
is Kleshchev, it is also Uglov by Lemma
5.7
(2), so that the generalised
(|M_{\ell}|,|M_{\ell-1}|,\dotsc,|M_{1}|)
-tableau
\mathsf{T}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
, defined by
\mathsf{T}^{\mu}(a,b)=b
-th least element of
M_{\ell-a+1}
, is column semistandard by Proposition
5.6
.
Let

i_{0}:=\mathrm{min}\{i\in[1,\,\ell]\mid|\mu^{(i)}|>0\},

and let
\mathfrak{m}
be the topmost removable node of
\mu^{(i_{0})}
.
Then for each
i\in[1,\,i_{0}-1]
,
\mu^{(i)}=\varnothing
so that
\beta_{r^{\prime}_{i}}(\mu^{(i)})=\mathbb{Z}_{<r^{\prime}_{i}}
.
Since
\mathsf{T}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
is column semistandard, we thus have

\mathbb{Z}_{<r^{\prime}_{i}}\subseteq\beta_{r^{\prime}_{i_{0}}}(\mu^{(i_{0})})
, so that

\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}}(\mathfrak{m})-1\geq\mathrm{min}(\mathbb{Z}\setminus\beta_{r^{\prime}_{i_{0}}}(\mu^{(i_{0})}))\geq r^{\prime}_{i}=\mathrm{max}(\beta_{r^{\prime}_{i}}(\mu^{(i)}))+1,

for all
i\in[1,\,i_{0}-1]
.
Together with Lemma
5.3
(2), we conclude that
there does not exist
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{AR}(\boldsymbol{\mu})
with
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}\succ_{\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}}\mathfrak{m}
.
Consequently, there does not exist
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{AR}(\boldsymbol{\mu})
such that
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}\rhd_{\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}}\mathfrak{m}

by Lemmas
5.3
(1) and
5.7
(1).
In particular,
\mathfrak{m}
is a good node of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
with respect to
\unrhd_{\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}}
.

Let
j=\mathsf{res}^{\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}}_{e}(\mathfrak{m})
, and let
\tilde{j}\in\mathbb{Z}/e\mathbb{Z}
with
\tilde{j}\equiv_{e}j-|\mathbf{y}^{B}|
.
By Lemma
5.3
(1),
j\not\equiv_{e}|\mathbf{y}^{B}|
, and so
\tilde{j}\neq 0
.
By Proposition
5.10
(1), there exists a bijection

\phi:\mathsf{AR}^{e,\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mu})\to\mathsf{AR}^{e,|\mathbf{u}|}_{\tilde{j}}(\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\mu}))
satisfying, for all

\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{AR}^{e,\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mu})
,

\phi(\mathfrak{n})
is removable if and only if
\mathfrak{n}
is removable, in which case
\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{-\mathfrak{n}})=(\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))^{-\phi(\mathfrak{n})}
, and

\phi(\mathfrak{n})\unrhd_{|\mathbf{u}|}\phi(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})
if and only if
\mathfrak{n}\unrhd_{\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}}\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
.
Thus,

\phi(\mathfrak{m})
is a good node of
\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\mu})
with respect to
\unrhd_{|\mathbf{u}|}
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\nu}=(\nu^{(1)},\dotsc,\nu^{(\ell)}):=\boldsymbol{\mu}^{-\mathfrak{m}}
.
Then
\beta_{r^{\prime}_{a}}(\nu^{(a)})=\beta_{r^{\prime}_{a}}(\mu^{(a)})=\mathbb{Z}_{<|\mathbf{y}^{B}|}\cup M_{a}
for all
a\in[1,\ell]\setminus\{i_{0}\}
,
while

\displaystyle\beta_{r^{\prime}_{i_{0}}}(\nu^{(i_{0})})

\displaystyle=\beta_{r^{\prime}_{i_{0}}}(\mu^{(i_{0})})\setminus\{\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}}(\mathfrak{m})\}\cup\{\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}}(\mathfrak{m})-1\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{Z}_{<|\mathbf{y}^{B}|}\cup(M_{i_{0}}\setminus\{\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}}(\mathfrak{m})\}\cup\{\mathsf{cont}_{\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}}(\mathfrak{m})-1\}).

From this, we conclude that the block
B^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{H}_{n-1}
in which
\boldsymbol{\nu}
lies is a core block with
\mathrm{mv}^{w_{0}}_{\ell}(B^{\prime})=0
.
Furthermore,

\mathbf{y}^{B^{\prime}}=\mathbf{y}^{B}
(while
z^{B^{\prime}}_{a}=z^{B}_{a}
for all
a\in[1,\,e]\setminus\{\overline{j},j+1\}
, and
z^{B^{\prime}}_{j+1}=z^{B}_{j+1}-1
and
z^{B^{\prime}}_{\overline{j}}=z^{B}_{\overline{j}}+1
).
Thus
B^{\prime}
also satisfies Condition (I) of being initial.
Consequently, by Proposition
5.1
, for all
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=(\lambda^{(1)},\dotsc,\lambda^{\ell})\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
lying in
B^{\prime}
and
i\in[1,\ell]
,

\beta_{r^{\prime}_{i}}(\lambda^{(i)})=\mathbb{Z}_{<|\mathbf{y}^{B}|}\cup L^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{i}
for some
L^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{i}\subseteq[|\mathbf{y}^{B}|,\,|\mathbf{y}^{B}|+e-1]
.
In particular,

\Psi_{\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}},\mathbf{u}}(f_{j}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}))=f_{\tilde{j}}(\Psi_{\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}},\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}))

(5.3)

for all
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
lying in
B^{\prime}
by Proposition
5.10
(2).
Note also that
\mathsf{AR}^{e,\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\nu})=\mathsf{AR}^{e,\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mu})
.

As
\mathfrak{m}
is the largest node (with respect to
\unrhd_{\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}}
) in
\mathsf{AR}^{e,\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mu})=\mathsf{AR}^{e,\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\nu})
,
we see that

f_{j}(G(\boldsymbol{\nu};\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}))=f_{j}(\boldsymbol{\nu};\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}})+\sum_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\lhd\boldsymbol{\nu}}d^{\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}}_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\boldsymbol{\nu}}(v)\,f_{j}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\lambda}},\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}})=(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}\\
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\lhd\boldsymbol{\mu}\end{subarray}}c_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(v)(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}})

where
c_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(v)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}[v,v^{-1}].

Consequently,

f_{j}(G(\boldsymbol{\nu};\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}))=G(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}\\
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\lhd\boldsymbol{\mu}\end{subarray}}a_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(v)G(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}})

where
a_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(v)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}[v,v^{-1}]
with
a_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(v^{-1})=a_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(v)
.
Applying
\Psi_{\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}},\mathbf{u}}
to this last equation and using (
5.3
), we get

\displaystyle f_{\tilde{j}}(\Psi_{\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}},\mathbf{u}}(G(\boldsymbol{\nu};\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}))=\Psi_{\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}},\mathbf{u}}(f_{j}(G(\boldsymbol{\nu};\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}})))=\Psi_{\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}},\mathbf{u}}(G(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}))+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}\\
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\lhd\boldsymbol{\mu}\end{subarray}}a_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(v)\,\Psi_{\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}}(G(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}})).

We then apply induction hypothesis to
\boldsymbol{\nu}
and
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
with
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\lhd\boldsymbol{\mu}
to get

\displaystyle f_{\tilde{j}}(G(\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\nu});|\mathbf{u}|))=\Psi_{\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}},\mathbf{u}}(G(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}))+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}\\
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\lhd\boldsymbol{\mu}\end{subarray}}a_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(v)\,G(\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda});|\mathbf{u}|)).

Since

\Psi_{\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}},\mathbf{u}}(G(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}))-(\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\mu});|\mathbf{u}|)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}\\
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\neq\boldsymbol{\mu}\end{subarray}}d^{\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\boldsymbol{\mu}}(v)\,(\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda});|\mathbf{u}|)\in\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{P}}v\mathbb{Z}[v](\lambda;|\mathbf{u}|)
,
and

\displaystyle\overline{\Psi_{\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}},\mathbf{u}}(G(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}))}

\displaystyle=\overline{f_{\tilde{j}}(G(\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\nu});|\mathbf{u}|))}-\overline{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}\\
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\lhd\boldsymbol{\mu}\end{subarray}}a_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(v)\,G(\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda});|\mathbf{u}|))}

\displaystyle=f_{\tilde{j}}(G(\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\nu});|\mathbf{u}|))-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}\\
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\lhd\boldsymbol{\mu}\end{subarray}}a_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(v)\,G(\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda});|\mathbf{u}|))

\displaystyle=\Psi_{\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}},\mathbf{u}}(G(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}})),

we see that
\Psi_{\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}},\mathbf{u}}(G(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}))
satisfies the characterising properties of
G(\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\mu});|\mathbf{u}|)
so that

\Psi_{\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}},\mathbf{u}}(G(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}))=G(\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\mu});|\mathbf{u}|),

as desired.
∎

Theorem 5.12
.

Let
B
be a core block of
\mathcal{H}_{n}
, and suppose that there exists
w\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
such that
\mathbf{r}^{w}\in\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{e}^{\ell}
and
\mathrm{mv}^{\mathbf{r}^{w}}_{\ell}(B)=0
.

(1)

For each
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
lying in
B
with
\iota_{\ell,e}((\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{r})^{w})=(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*};\mathbf{r}^{*}_{B})
,
there exists a unique
\Omega(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
such that

\iota_{\ell,e}((\Omega(\boldsymbol{\lambda});\mathbf{r})^{w})=((\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*})^{\sigma_{B}\tau_{B}};\mathbf{v}_{e}(|\mathbf{y}^{B}|)\ell+\mathbf{Sc}(B))^{\rho_{e}^{-j_{B}}},

where
\rho_{e}=(1,2,\dotsc,e)\in\mathfrak{S}_{e}
.
(Recall the notation of
\mathbf{v}_{e}(x)
in Subsection
2.1
.)

(2)

Let
\mathbf{u}:=\mathbf{r}^{w}-|\mathbf{y}^{B}|\boldsymbol{1}
, and for each
\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
, define
\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\nu}):=\beta^{-1}(\mathbf{U}(\beta_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\nu})))
.

(a)

If
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
lying in
B
such that
(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{r})
is Kleshchev, then
\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\Omega(\boldsymbol{\mu}))
is
e
-regular.

(b)

There exists a
\mathbb{C}(v)
-linear map
\Theta:\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{r}}\to\mathcal{F}_{|\mathbf{u}|}
such that

\Theta(\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{r})=(\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\Omega(\boldsymbol{\lambda})),|\mathbf{u}|)
for all
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
lying in
B
, and

\Theta(G(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{r}))=G(\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\Omega(\boldsymbol{\mu})),|\mathbf{u}|)

for all
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
lying in
B
such that
(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{r})
is Kleshchev.

In particular, for all
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
lying in
B
such that
(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{r})
is Kleshchev,

d^{\mathbf{r}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\boldsymbol{\mu}}(v)=d_{\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\Omega(\boldsymbol{\lambda})),\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\Omega(\boldsymbol{\mu}))}(v)

for all
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
lying in
B
, and
d_{\lambda,\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\Omega(\boldsymbol{\mu}))}(v)=0
if
\lambda\neq\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\Omega(\boldsymbol{\nu}))
for any
\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
lying in
B
.

Proof.

Since
\mathbf{r}^{w}\in\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{e}^{\ell}
and
\mathrm{mv}^{\mathbf{r}^{w}}_{\ell}(B)=0
, we may choose
w_{0}
to be
w
.

By Proposition
4.22
and Lemma
4.21
, the unique initial core block
B_{0}
that is Scopes equivalent to
B
has
\mathbf{r}^{*}_{B_{0}}=(\mathbf{v}_{e}(|\mathbf{y}^{B}|)\ell+\mathbf{Sc}(B))^{\rho_{e}^{-j_{B}}}
.
Furthermore, each
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
lying in
B
with
\iota_{\ell,e}((\boldsymbol{\lambda};\mathbf{r})^{w_{0}})=(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*},\mathbf{r}^{*}_{B})
corresponds bijectively to a
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
lying in
B_{0}
satisfying

\displaystyle\iota_{\ell,e}((\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{r})^{w_{0}})=((\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*})^{\sigma_{B}\tau_{B}\rho_{e}^{-j_{B}}};\mathbf{r}^{*}_{B_{0}})=((\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*})^{\sigma_{B}\tau_{B}};\mathbf{v}_{e}(|\mathbf{y}^{B}|)\ell+\mathbf{Sc}(B))^{\rho_{e}^{-j_{B}}}.

Thus
\Omega(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
exists, and is unique since
\iota_{\ell,e}
is bijective between
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}\times\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
and
\mathcal{P}^{e}\times\mathbb{Z}^{e}
. This shows part (1).

Since
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\mapsto\Omega(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
is the map induced by the Scopes equivalence between
B
and
B_{0}
,
we have in particular that
for

\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
lying in
B
,
(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{r})
is Kleshchev if and only if
(\Omega(\boldsymbol{\mu});\mathbf{r})
is. Furthermore,

d^{\mathbf{r}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\boldsymbol{\mu}}(v)=d^{\mathbf{r}}_{\Omega(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\Omega(\boldsymbol{\mu})}(v)
for all
\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\mu}
lying
B
with
(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{r})
Kleshchev (see
[
LM02
, Theorem 20]
for a proof when
\ell=1
; the proof for
\ell>1
is similar).
In other words, if
\Xi
is a
\mathbb{C}(v)
-linear endomorphism of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{r}}
sending
(\boldsymbol{\nu};\mathbf{r})
to
(\Omega(\boldsymbol{\nu});\mathbf{r})
for all
\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
lying
B
, then we get
\Xi(G(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{r}))=G(\Omega(\boldsymbol{\mu});\mathbf{r})
for all
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
lying in
B
such that
(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{r})
is Kleshchev.

As
|\mathbf{y}^{B_{0}}|=|\mathbf{y}^{B}|
by Lemma
4.7
, we have
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}-|\mathbf{y}^{B}|\boldsymbol{1}=\mathbf{r}^{w_{0}}-|\mathbf{y}^{B_{0}}|\boldsymbol{1}
.
Let
\Theta:\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{r}}\to\mathcal{F}_{|\mathbf{u}|}
be the map obtained by composing the above map
\Xi
with
\Psi_{\mathbf{r},\mathbf{u}}
from Proposition
5.11
.
For
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}
lying in
B
such that
(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{r})
is Kleshchev,
(\Omega(\boldsymbol{\mu});\mathbf{r})
is Kleshchev, and applying Proposition
5.11
, we conclude that

\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\Omega(\boldsymbol{\mu}))
is
e
-regular, and that

\Theta(G(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{r}))=\Psi_{\mathbf{r},\mathbf{u}}(\Xi(G(\boldsymbol{\mu};\mathbf{r})))=\Psi_{\mathbf{r},\mathbf{u}}(G(\Omega(\boldsymbol{\mu});\mathbf{r}))=G(\Phi_{\mathbf{u}}(\Omega(\boldsymbol{\mu}));|\mathbf{u}|),

proving part (2).
∎

Remark 5.13
.

Applying Ariki’s (resp. Brundan-Kleshchev’s) theorem to Theorem
5.12
yields the corresponding decomposition numbers (resp. graded decomposition numbers) of all core blocks of Ariki-Koike algebras satisfying the conditions in Theorem
5.12
in terms of those of the Iwahori-Hecke algebras of type
A
when
\mathrm{char}(\mathbb{F})=0
.
